account, the cleanup keeps an account's newest entries and drops only the
older ones, and an account within the bound loses none, however old. The
IT caught an off-by-one in the first version of that query, which kept one
entry too many. `SmtpMessageServiceTest` stubs the kept recipients by the
row's account and Message-ID, so the send finds them through those alone,
and refuses a copy with an added Bcc. Neither was run against the old code:
the API both call changed with it.

**Reopened at 1.31** (Medium, from the verification pass; read, not
measured). The key holds. The save keeps the entry under the Message-ID it
minted (`<uuid@voxrox.org>`), the sync writes a row's Message-ID as the
server's ENVELOPE reports it, and the send looks the entry up by the row's
account and Message-ID, so a draft the server moves to another folder with
its Message-ID intact is still checked against what was typed. The count
does not hold as written. A save forgets the entry of the revision it
replaces only when it finds that revision's row, in the account's current
Drafts folder, and the server then deletes it (`saveDraftAsync`); otherwise it
adds its own entry and forgets none. All three conditions are the server's.
Without APPENDUID the replaced revision has no row until a sync writes one,
and autosaves come 3 s after the user pauses (`STEADY_SAVE_DEBOUNCE_MS` in
`frontend/src/lib/compose/session.ts`) while the sync runs every 5 min; a
moved `\Drafts` role makes `isReplaceableDraft` refuse the old row; a refused
delete skips the forget. So the entries grow by saves, not by drafts, and a
server that hides one draft and withholds APPENDUID has that draft's entry
pushed out by a thousand of the user's autosaves — hours of composing, spread
over however long the server cares to wait — and then presents it with a Bcc
added: the case the 1.20 and 1.29 fixes were written to close. An honest
server without UIDPLUS inflates the count the same way, and keeps each
superseded revision the next save came too early to find, since the save
cannot address it to delete it.
**Recommendation:** a bound the server's answers cannot drive — entries that
grow by drafts whatever the server answers, for instance retiring a replaced
revision's entry by what this client minted for it rather than by its row, or
a bound saves cannot plausibly reach; which, at fix time.

**Fixed again at 1.36** (2026-10-05, #632), by the first of the two:
entries that grow by drafts whatever the server answers. Each entry now also
carries the stableId the save minted with its Message-ID
(`draft_recipients.stable_id`), which is what the next save of the same
draft names as the revision it replaces. Once the new revision is stored,
`DraftPersistenceService.markReplacedRevision` marks that entry superseded
(`superseded_at`) by the stableId alone — whether the save found the old
revision's row, and whether the server let it delete it, no longer decides.
The entry is not deleted: the server may keep that revision and the user may
still send it, and it is still checked against what was typed. Current and
superseded entries are bounded apart, a thousand of each
(`deleteCurrentButNewest`, `deleteSupersededButNewest`), so autosaves push
out only each other's superseded entries, and a hidden draft's current entry
goes only after the user has started some thousand newer drafts. Where the
server does delete the replaced revision, its entry is still dropped at once,
as before. The schema change is to `V1__init.sql` in place, before the first
public release, with the pinned checksum moved.

**Dynamic verification.** `DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` runs the attack in
the service's order against SQLite, under a bound of three: a hidden draft's
entry, then ten saves of another draft. With the mark, the hidden entry and
the newest revision are the current ones, and the superseded ones stay within
the bound; without it — the save of 1.29 against a server that gives it no
row — the hidden entry is pushed out. It also checks that a mark touches only
the named, current entry of the account, and that each kind is pruned on its
own. `DraftPersistenceServiceTest` checks that a save marks the revision it
replaces when the server gave it no row to delete, and marks nothing when the
append fails or nothing is replaced; with the mark removed it fails, which was
run and seen.

**Residual.** A revision the server keeps after it was replaced is checked
against what was typed until a thousand newer superseded entries push its
entry out, and then like one composed elsewhere. A draft being saved holds
two current entries for the moment of the save, so the current bound counts
some 999 drafts. The earlier residual stands: a draft the server presents
under a Message-ID of its own is checked like one composed elsewhere.

**A gap, found by the pass over 1.37 and fixed at 1.39** (2026-10-05, #635). The
entry is kept before the APPEND, as it must be, and the replaced revision is
marked superseded only after the new one is stored. A save whose APPEND the
server rejects therefore left its own entry current and the previous
revision's current too: a server rejecting every APPEND grew the current
entries by one per autosave — the frontend keeps saving, since the 202
returns before the outcome — and after some thousand pushed out a hidden
draft's entry, the attack of 1.31 by another route. Its precondition is
visible, as every rejected save records `DRAFT_SAVE_FAILED`. Read from the
code ordering, not measured end to end. `appendDraftMessage` now sets the
new revision's entry aside (`DraftPersistenceService.setAside`, which until 1.40
also marked the replaced revision after a stored one, `markReplacedRevision`
until 1.38) whenever the APPEND does not store it, by a `false` outcome or an
exception, so the previous revision stays the draft's one current entry. Set
aside, not dropped: a server that stored the revision after all and presents
it later still has it checked against what was typed. The recovery draft
after a failed send shares the path. `DraftPersistenceServiceTest` checks
both failure shapes; with the set-aside removed both fail, which was run and
seen. `DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` runs ten rejected saves under a bound of
three: the hidden draft and the last stored revision stay the current ones.
The rest of the 1.36 fix the pass over 1.37 confirmed: the mark does not depend on the
server's answers, the two kinds are pruned apart, and the send looks an entry
up by account and Message-ID whether current or superseded.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.39** (a separate session's).
`appendDraftMessage` sets the new revision's entry aside in a `finally`
whenever the APPEND did not store it, by a `false` outcome or an exception,
and `markSuperseded` touches only a current entry. With the set-aside
removed, `appendFailureKeepsOldDraft` and `aFailingAppendSetsTheNewEntryAside`
fail, which was run and seen. The recovery draft shares the path.

**A gap, found by the pass over 1.39 and fixed at 1.40** (2026-10-05, #638).
What 1.36 retires is the revision a save _names_, and the client names the
stableId the previous save returned (`saveOnce` in
`frontend/src/lib/compose/session.ts`), which the 202 returns before the
APPEND's outcome. After a rejected save the next one therefore names the
rejected revision, already set aside, and the last stored revision is named
by no save: it stays current, and since the rejected revision has no row,
the save does not delete the stored one from the server either. A server
rejecting every other APPEND left one more current entry per two autosaves,
and after some two thousand pushed out a hidden draft's entry — the attack
of 1.31 by a third route. Its precondition is visible, as half the saves
record `DRAFT_SAVE_FAILED`. Measured, not read: `DraftRecipientsChainIT`
drives the real `DraftPersistenceService` against SQLite through a hidden
draft and ten saves of another, stored and rejected in turn, each naming
`replaces` as the client does. Against the 1.39 code the current entries
are the hidden draft and all five stored revisions, which was run and seen.

Each entry now carries a chain (`draft_recipients.chain_id`): the stableId
of the draft's first revision saved here. A save inherits it from the entry
of the revision it names (`DraftRecipientsRepository.findChainId`), or
starts a chain with its own stableId when it names none or one with no
entry here. A stored save sets aside every current entry of its chain saved
no later than itself (`supersedeEarlierInChain`), whichever revision it
named; a rejected save still sets aside its own. A draft therefore keeps one
current entry whatever the server answers to its APPENDs, and only a
revision of the chain saved after the stored one — a slow save a later one
overtook — stays current beside it. The schema change is to `V1__init.sql`
in place, with the pinned checksum moved.

**Dynamic verification (1.40).** `DraftRecipientsChainIT` passes: the
hidden draft and the last stored revision are the only current entries.
`DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` checks that the set-aside touches only the
account's earlier current entries of the chain — not the stored revision, a
newer one, another draft's chain or another account's entries, nor the time
of an entry already set aside — and that a chain is found by account and
stableId only. `DraftPersistenceServiceTest` checks that a stored save
inherits the named revision's chain and sets aside its earlier revisions,
that a failed one sets aside only its own, and that a first save starts its
own chain.

**Residual.** A save whose chain lookup fails — the database read errs, or
the named revision's entry is gone — starts a new chain, and the draft's
earlier revisions keep their current entries; neither is the server's to
cause. A draft the user reopens from a row the server wrote under a stableId
of its own starts a new chain as well, one per such reopening. After a
rejected save the last stored revision also stays on the server as a second
copy of the draft, since the next save looks for the rejected revision's row
to delete and finds none; that copy's entry is set aside, so it is checked
against what was typed until newer superseded entries push it out. **Fixed
at 1.68** (#670): a stored save deletes, besides the revision it names, every
earlier revision of its chain the server still holds a row for
(`DraftRecipientsRepository.findEarlierRevisions`, then each row through the
guard the named one passes, `isReplaceableDraft`), and a send does the same for
the draft it supersedes, whose last stored revision stayed in Drafts after the
message went out by the same route. Earlier by `saved_seq`, so a later revision
whose save ran first stays, and only this client's chain, so a draft composed
elsewhere, which has no entry here, is never touched. The entries are not: a
deleted revision's entry stays, set aside, as 1.58 requires. A failed lookup
leaves the copies for the next save rather than failing this one. The client
composes one draft at a time (its compose session is a module singleton), so
no earlier revision of a chain is another window's newer content.
`DraftRecipientsChainIT` `aSaveAfterARejectedOneLeavesNoSecondCopy` and
`aSendAfterARejectedSaveLeavesNoDraftBehind` fail without the chain's lookup
on their path, `aSaveDeletesOnlyEarlierRevisionsOfItsOwnDraft` and
`DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT.findsTheEarlierRevisionsOfTheChain` without the
query's order or its chain, all run and seen. Functional; no claim of B1-5's
changes.

**Reopened at 1.41** (Medium, from the independent pass over 1.40, a
separate agent's; measured with the production executor and SQLite
settings, the IMAP side stubbed). What 1.40 states holds as written.
`findChainId` reads the named revision's chain by account and stableId, a
save naming none or one with no entry starts its own, a stored save calls
`supersedeEarlierInChain` and a failed one only `markSuperseded` on its own
stableId. Against the 1.39 code `DraftRecipientsChainIT` leaves the hidden
draft and all five stored revisions current, which was run and seen. With
the stored save's set-aside given another chain, or with the named
revision's chain discarded, it fails with those six current again, and so
does `aSaveSetsAsideTheEarlierRevisionsWithoutTheirRow`; with the failed
save's set-aside pointed elsewhere it fails with the rejected last revision
current, as do the two tests 1.39 names. Each condition of the set-aside
query — account, chain, still current, not the stored revision, saved no
later — and the account condition of `findChainId` was removed in turn, and
a test of `DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` failed each time. `saved_at` is
stored as epoch milliseconds and the bound time the same way, so the
comparison is consistent.

The chain does not hold whatever the server does, and the 1.40 residual's
"neither is the server's to cause" is too early, for two reasons:

- **A save looks up its chain before the save it names need have written
  its entry.** The 202 returns the stableId before the task has run, and
  the client names it in its next save; `chainOf` runs at the start of that
  save, `keepTypedRecipients` partway into the one it names. The 3 s
  debounce ordinarily hides the gap. The `userMailExecutor` gate does not:
  its eight permits are held by saves that kept their entries and wait for
  the BACKGROUND lane, so while a server holds that lane — a slow APPEND, or
  a sync command it keeps alive — every further save parks before its keep,
  and the parked saves start one per finished APPEND once it lets go. When
  it answers each APPEND faster than a parked save writes its entry, the
  next save's lookup finds nothing and starts a chain of its own, and the
  save it named stays current with no later save in its chain: one more
  current entry each time, and nothing recorded, since every APPEND is
  stored. A throwaway IT drove the real service through
  `new AsyncConfig().userMailExecutor()`, with a fair lock standing for the
  lane and the production pool of four over WAL: forty saves, each naming
  the one before, left 15–31 extra current entries for one draft when the
  held APPENDs were then answered at once, 5–15 at 1–2 ms per APPEND, and
  none at 5 ms or more (Ryzen 9 9900X; slower writes widen the window). The
  1.39 code, which marked the named revision after the APPEND, left 0–4
  under the same burst, so 1.40 widened the window rather than opened it.
  Whether a server reaches those speeds over a real connection, four or so
  round trips per APPEND unless it sends its answers ahead of the commands,
  was not measured; a server on the user's LAN could.
- **The entry's write fails at once under a concurrent writer.**
  `keepTypedRecipients` writes through `save`, which for an assigned key is
  a merge: a SELECT, then the INSERT in the same transaction. A connection
  that has begun reading and then asks to write is refused rather than kept
  waiting, so if another connection holds the write lock, or has committed
  since the SELECT, the INSERT fails with `SQLITE_BUSY` or
  `SQLITE_BUSY_SNAPSHOT` without waiting out `busy_timeout` (observed:
  within a third of a second of the save starting, against five seconds).
  That revision then has no entry, and an untouched send of it is checked
  against the row, the case 1.20 set out to close; the next save's lookup
  misses as well.
  Forty sequential saves beside a connection writing in short transactions
  lost no entry at 2 ms held in every 22, one at 5 in 10, and ten at 20 in 25. In production that writer is a sync's batch transaction, whose length
  the server's data drives (read, not measured). Not new at 1.40: the entry
  has been written this way since 1.20.

Both are less likely than the 1.39 and 1.40 routes, which needed only
rejections: the first needs a held lane and fast answers, the second a
write load at the draft's last save. Severity stays **Medium**, the
finding's own, as at every reopening of B1-5; a server that presents the
draft under a Message-ID of its own, the standing residual, needs neither.
Separately, and within the 1.36 design, the current entries also grow by
every compose the user discards, which forgets no entry, and by every send
whose clean-up finds no row for the draft — nearly every send, against a
server withholding APPENDUID — so the `KEPT_DRAFT_RECIPIENTS` javadoc's
"neither sent nor deleted by this client" overstates what they count.
**Recommendation:** write the entry, with its chain, before the 202 rather
than in the task, by a write that does not upgrade a read (a plain INSERT,
or a transaction begun IMMEDIATE), and do not append a revision whose entry
could not be written, so every stableId the client can name has an entry
and every draft saved here has one to be checked against. Ordering "saved
no later" by insertion rather than by `LocalDateTime.now()` would also take
wall-clock steps out of the set-aside: after a DST fall-back or a clock
correction the earlier revision stays current until a save passes its time
(read).

**Fixed again at 1.42** (2026-10-06, #641), as recommended.
`DraftController.saveDraft` calls `DraftPersistenceService.acceptDraftSave`
before it answers: that mints the identity, looks up the chain of the
revision the save names and writes the entry, synchronously, so the stableId
the 202 hands the client has its entry, with its chain, before the client can
name it. The async task no longer writes one. The entry is written by
`DraftRecipientsRepository.insertEntry`, a plain INSERT whose transaction
begins with the write, so it waits out `busy_timeout` like any other writer
instead of upgrading a read. An entry that cannot be written, or a chain that
cannot be looked up, fails the request before anything is dispatched, so no
revision is appended without an entry; the save is reported failed, not
stored unchecked. The one exception is the recovery draft a failed send parks
(`saveRecoveryDraft`): it is the only copy of what the user wrote, so it is
appended even when its entry fails, and logged. "Newest" and "saved no later"
go by `draft_recipients.saved_seq`, one more than the table's largest, taken
inside the same INSERT, rather than by `saved_at`, so a clock step no longer
reorders the set-aside or the bounds. The `KEPT_DRAFT_RECIPIENTS` javadoc now
names what the current entries count, discarded composes and sends without a
row among them. `V1__init.sql` is edited in place again, the pinned checksum
moved.

**Dynamic verification (1.42).** `DraftRecipientsChainIT` now runs on the
production pragmas and pool (WAL, four connections); until 1.42 it inherited
the IT profile's single pooled connection without WAL, under which a second
writer waits for the pool and no SQLite lock conflict can arise.
`theChainHoldsBeforeTheNamedSaveRuns` accepts five saves, each naming the one
before, before any task runs, then runs the tasks newest first: one current
entry for the draft, one chain. With the entry written in the task again it
fails, run and seen. `theEntryWaitsOutAConcurrentWriter` has another
connection hold the write lock while the entry is written: the write waits and
lands once that connection commits. A read-then-write in its place fails with
`SQLITE_BUSY` at once, run and seen; on the old single-connection setup it
passed, which is why the setup changed. `DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` checks
that the set-aside and the bounds go by the order written, with an entry
written later under an earlier `saved_at`. The 1.41 burst through the real
`userMailExecutor` was not rerun: what it exploited, a lookup before the
named entry exists, cannot occur while the entry is written before the 202
(read).

**Residual.** A save's chain lookup still misses when the named revision's
entry is gone — pushed out by the bound, or a stableId the server minted —
and starts a new chain, as at 1.40; that is no longer the server's timing to
cause. Every save now writes to SQLite on the request thread, so a database
held past `busy_timeout` fails the save visibly where it used to drop the
check silently.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.42** (a separate session's; read from the
code at `7635e5f` and measured with the IMAP side stubbed, the production
executor, pragmas and pool real). `DraftController.saveDraft` calls
`acceptDraftSave` before it dispatches, and the task writes no entry. The
entry is written by `insertEntry`, a native INSERT in a transaction of its
own: neither the controller nor `DraftPersistenceService` is transactional
and `spring.jpa.open-in-view` is off, so nothing reads before it in that
transaction. A chain lookup that throws, or an entry that cannot be written,
leaves `acceptDraftSave` before `saveDraftAsync` is called (read; no test
drives that through the controller), and `saveRecoveryDraft` catches its
entry's failure and appends anyway. Against that code
`DraftRecipientsChainIT`, `DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT`,
`DraftPersistenceServiceTest`, `DraftControllerTest` and
`SmtpMessageServiceTest` pass. With the entry written in the task again
`theChainHoldsBeforeTheNamedSaveRuns` fails, and with "earlier" and "newest"
by `saved_at` again `earlierGoesByTheOrderWrittenNotTheClock` and
`newestGoesByTheOrderWritten` fail, all run and seen. A read and then
`insertEntry` in one transaction, beside a connection holding the write
lock, failed with `SQLITE_BUSY` after 23 ms, where `insertEntry` alone
waits. The 1.41 burst was rerun through `new AsyncConfig().userMailExecutor()`
with a fair lock for the lane: forty saves, each naming the one before,
accepted while the lane was held, then answered at once or at 1, 2 or 5 ms,
all stored or every other one rejected, with and without a connection
holding the write lock for 5 ms in every 6. All twenty-four runs ended with
forty entries, one of them current, one chain, and no save refused at
acceptance (Core i7-1255U). `saved_seq` is taken by a subquery of the
INSERT, which runs under the write lock, so no two entries share one, and a
new entry is above every entry left, whatever was deleted before it.

**Reopened at 1.43** (Medium, from the same pass; measured). 1.42 moved the
entry's write before the dispatch and left its set-aside where 1.39 put it,
around the APPEND in `appendDraftMessage`. Until 1.42 the entry was written
just before that APPEND, so the set-aside covered every save that had an
entry. Now the task does three things before it that can fail — it looks up
the row of the revision it replaces, then the account, then builds the
message — and a task that fails there leaves its entry current, set aside by
nothing. The next save names it, since the 202 returned its stableId, and
joins its chain; if that save fails the same way the chain only grows, as no
stored save comes to set those entries aside. Honest mail takes this route. A
reply carries the original's `References` into its own, the sync stores a
folded header with its CRLF, and the builder refuses a line break (§3b), so
every save of a reply to such a message fails in the build. Measured over
GreenMail through the real sync, `MailFacade.prepareReply` and
`DraftPersistenceService`: five saves of one reply, each naming the one
before, each recorded `DRAFT_SAVE_FAILED` and left its entry current — five
current entries in one chain, and no draft on the server. With the builder
stubbed to refuse, fifty such saves beside a hidden draft left fifty-one
current entries, the hidden one among them. Every autosave of such a reply
adds one, so after some thousand a hidden draft's entry is pushed out: the
1.39 route by another way, with its visible precondition, since each failed
save records `DRAFT_SAVE_FAILED`. A hostile server needs only to deliver a
message with a folded `References` and wait for the user to answer it. The
recovery draft shares the gap: `saveRecoveryDraft` keeps its entry first,
and a build that fails in `appendDraftMessage` is caught there and leaves
it current (read). A task that never runs — the sidecar closing with saves
parked on the gate or the lane — leaves its entry current as well; that is
the user's own exit and a few entries at most (read).
**Recommendation:** set the entry aside on every path out of the task that
does not store the revision, not only around the APPEND — in
`saveDraftAsync` and `saveRecoveryDraft`, keyed on whether the APPEND
stored it. Separately, unfold `In-Reply-To` and `References` where they
are read off the wire, which closes the honest route and the lost replies of
§3b.

**Fixed again at 1.44** (2026-10-06, #644), as recommended.
`saveDraftAsync` sets the entry aside in a `finally` unless the task stored the
revision, whichever way it leaves: a `false` outcome, an exception from the
APPEND, or one from before it — the replaced row's lookup, the account, the
message build. `appendDraftMessage` no longer sets anything aside, so the
rule has one owner in each pipeline, and `saveRecoveryDraft` applies it in a
`finally` of its own. A task whose APPEND was stored and whose local
bookkeeping then failed sets its entry aside as well, where it used to stay
current; the entry stays, and the send still finds it by Message-ID. The
folded header the reopening ran through is a fix of its own (§3b).

**Dynamic verification (1.44).** `DraftRecipientsChainIT` saves a hidden
draft and then ten revisions of another whose message build fails, each
naming the one before: only the hidden draft's entry is current. Against the
1.43 code it fails with all eleven current, run and seen.
`DraftPersistenceServiceTest` checks the set-aside for a save whose build
fails, one whose replaced-row lookup fails, and a recovery draft whose build
fails; all three fail against the 1.43 code, run and seen. The stored path
still sets nothing of its own aside
(`aSaveSetsAsideTheEarlierRevisionsWithoutTheirRow`).

**Residual.** A task that never runs — the sidecar closing with saves parked
on the gate or the lane — still leaves its entry current, a few entries per
such exit (1.43).

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.44** (a separate agent's; read from the code
at `065920b` and measured with the IMAP side stubbed and the production
executor, pragmas and pool real, and through the real stack over GreenMail
and over a scripted TLS server). `saveDraftAsync` sets the save's entry aside
in its `finally` unless the task stored the revision, and `stored` turns true
only once `appendDraftMessage` has returned true, which it does only after an
APPEND the server answered as stored. A throwaway probe took every way out:
the replaced row's lookup, the account, the message build, a `false` APPEND
outcome, an `Error` from the APPEND, an `Error` from the local upsert or from
the chain's set-aside after a stored APPEND, and a failed APPEND whose
`last_error` write then throws each set the entry aside exactly once, and a
stored save sets nothing of its own aside, also when the `last_error` clear
after it throws. Against the 1.43 code seven of those set nothing aside: the
three before the APPEND, the two `Error`s after a stored one, and a recovery
draft's failed build and its `Error` after a stored APPEND. Nothing in
`appendDraftMessage` sets anything aside: with the `finally` of
`saveDraftAsync` emptied, `appendFailureKeepsOldDraft`,
`aFailingAppendSetsTheNewEntryAside` and
`aRejectedSaveDoesNotLeaveTheStoredRevisionCurrent` fail beside the three
save tests 1.44 added, and with a set-aside put back into `appendDraftMessage`,
`appendFailureKeepsOldDraft` fails on a second `markSuperseded`, so each
pipeline has the one owner 1.44 states. With the `finally` of
`saveRecoveryDraft` emptied,
`saveRecoveryDraftSetsItsEntryAsideWhenTheBuildFails` fails. With `stored`
set before the APPEND, the tests of a failed APPEND and a failed build fail;
with it never set, `aSaveSetsAsideTheEarlierRevisionsWithoutTheirRow` and
three of the four in `DraftRecipientsChainIT` fail. Against the 1.43 code
`savesThatCannotBeBuiltLeaveNoCurrentEntry` fails with all eleven entries
current, and so do the three `DraftPersistenceServiceTest` cases 1.44 names,
all run and seen. Two gaps in the committed tests, from the same runs: no
test covers a recovery draft whose APPEND is refused, and a stored recovery
draft set aside by mistake fails only the throwaway probe.

The stored-then-failed case holds as 1.44 states it, with one precision: the
local upsert and the chain's set-aside each catch every `Exception`, so only
an `Error` leaves them, and an exception there is logged and the save counts
as stored, its entry current — right for a revision the server holds. Run
through the real `SmtpMessageService.sendDraftAsync` over SQLite: the stored
revision whose upsert threw a `StackOverflowError` has its entry set aside,
the revision before it stays the draft's current one, and the untouched send
of the stored revision is refused with `DRAFT_CHANGED_ON_SERVER` when the
server's copy adds a Bcc and goes out when the copy matches. Over GreenMail
through the real sync, a reply whose subject decodes to a line break (§3b)
fails every save in the build, and three such saves leave no entry current,
where the 1.43 code left all three current: until 1.44 that was mail any
sender can write reaching the 1.43 route after 1.45 closed the folded header.

**Reopened at 1.46** (Medium, from the same pass; measured). Since 1.42 the
entry is written when the save is accepted, and it is current until the
save's task settles it. 1.44 made every way out of the task settle it, but a
task that waits has not left, and `keepTypedRecipients` prunes the account's
current entries to the newest 999 on every acceptance. A save's task waits
for the account's BACKGROUND lane, which its APPEND takes, and once eight
wait there, for a permit of the `userMailExecutor` gate. A server that holds
that lane therefore keeps every later save waiting with a current entry, and
each autosave adds one: some thousand during one hold push out a hidden
draft's entry, though every APPEND is then stored and nothing is recorded,
since every save was answered 202 and none fails. Until 1.42 the entry was
written inside the task, after the gate's permit, so at most the eight
holding one could wait with an entry (read). The 1.43 rerun of the 1.41
burst counted the entries once the held saves had run, when the chain had
collapsed to one, not while they waited. Measured with a fair lock standing
for the lane, the production executor (`new AsyncConfig().userMailExecutor()`)
and SQLite with the production pragmas and pool: a hidden draft, then a
thousand saves of another, each naming the one before, accepted while the
lock was held. The hidden draft's entry was deleted at the thousandth
acceptance, with a thousand entries current and no save run; released, every
save stored and the draft settled to one current entry, the hidden one gone
for good, and no failure was recorded. With 999 saves the hidden entry
survives. Accepting the thousand took 4.3 s (Core i7-1255U): the pace is the
user's autosaves, one 3 s after each pause, not the app's.

That a server can hold the lane that long was measured too, through the real
`ImapAppendService.appendDraft` over TLS against a throwaway scripted server
built like `HostileImapServer`, with the production read timeout of 60 s. An
APPEND whose continuation the server put off by sending `* OK` every 20 s
returned stored after 140 s, and a second BACKGROUND-lane action asked for
3 s in waited 137 s. The read timeout bounds one read, not the command, and
Angus discards untagged responses while it waits for a continuation; what
bounds the command is B1-8's budget (`MAX_COMMAND_BYTES`, 64 MiB at
`RESPONSE_OVERHEAD_BYTES` = 280 a response), which such a trickle takes
months to spend (read). The account's sync waits on the same lane, and the
drafts reach the server only when the hold ends: that, not a recorded
failure, is what the user can see.

An exit makes those entries permanent, and the 1.44 residual's "a few entries
per such exit" does not hold. The sidecar is killed outright on quit, reload
and update (`stopBackendSidecar` in `frontend/src/lib/backend/sidecar.ts`,
read), so every save accepted and not yet settled keeps its current entry:
twenty saves accepted while the lane was held left twenty-one entries
current with the hidden draft. A graceful close, the parent watchdog's
`System.exit`, interrupts the saves parked on the gate before they run
(`GatedTaskExecutor`): twelve of the twenty never ran, and the eight on the
lane wait on a lock whose wait an interrupt does not end. A dispatch refused by a
closed executor (`TaskRejectedException`) leaves its entry current as well,
but no 202 goes out, and it happens only while the sidecar shuts down
(measured). What an exit leaves is therefore as many entries as the saves
accepted since the server took the lane, and they add up across sessions,
though the drafts of a session whose saves never reach the server are lost,
which the user would notice.

It asks more of the server than the routes of 1.39 and 1.43, which needed
only rejections or one kind of reply: a hold for as long as the user takes to
autosave some thousand times, hours of composing, or as many held saves
across exits. Severity stays **Medium**, the finding's own, as at every
reopening. **Recommendation:** let an entry count as current only once its
task has stored the revision — write it at acceptance as pending, found by
the send through its Message-ID and by the next save through its stableId
as now, but bounded with the superseded entries rather than the current
ones, and make it current where a stored save sets aside its chain's earlier
revisions. A save that waits, never runs or stops on any way out then holds
no current place, and the set-aside of 1.44 becomes the default rather than a
rule each way out has to reach. Bounding how many accepted saves an account
may have waiting is the other way; which, at fix time.

**Fixed again at 1.47** (2026-10-06, #648), by the first of the two ways
1.46 recommended. The entry is still written when the save is accepted, but
only as accepted: `draft_recipients.stored_at` stays empty, and the entry
takes a current place only once the server has stored its revision.
`DraftRecipientsRepository.markStored`, which `appendDraftMessage` calls
right after a stored APPEND, sets `stored_at` on the revision's entry and,
in the same statement, sets aside every earlier entry of its chain not set
aside already — current or still accepted — so a draft never holds two
current entries. The two bounds split by that state: current entries,
stored and not set aside, on one side, and everything else — set aside, or
accepted and not stored — on the other, a thousand of each as before
(`deleteCurrentButNewest`, `deleteNotCurrentButNewest`). A save that waits
for the lane, never runs, or stops on any way out therefore holds no current
place, and nothing has to reach it to keep it out: the `finally` blocks 1.44
put in `saveDraftAsync` and `saveRecoveryDraft` are gone, and
`markSuperseded` with them. Two things follow from the same statement. An
earlier revision whose save was still waiting when a later one was stored is
set aside with the stored ones and stays so when its own APPEND is stored at
last; until 1.47 its entry stood current beside the later one's while it
waited. And a stored recovery draft becomes current through the same call,
in a chain of its own. The send is unchanged: it looks an entry up by
Message-ID whatever its state. `V1__init.sql` is edited in place, with the
pinned checksum moved.

**Dynamic verification (1.47).**
`DraftRecipientsChainIT.savesWaitingForTheLaneTakeNoCurrentPlace` saves a
hidden draft, then accepts a thousand saves of another
(`KEPT_DRAFT_RECIPIENTS`), each naming the one before, before any of them
runs, as while a server holds the lane, and then runs them in order. The
hidden draft's entry survives every acceptance and is the only current one
while they wait; once they have run, it and the last revision are the
current ones. Against the 1.44 code it fails at the thousandth acceptance,
with the hidden entry deleted, run and seen. Each part of the fix was then
neutralized in turn, each run and seen:

- An accepted entry written as stored, current from acceptance as before
  1.47, fails eight tests across `DraftRecipientsChainIT` and
  `DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT`: the 1.46 test, and also those of the 1.37,
  1.39 and 1.43 routes (`rejectedSavesDoNotPushOutAnotherDraft`,
  `aRejectedSaveDoesNotLeaveTheStoredRevisionCurrent`,
  `savesThatCannotBeBuiltLeaveNoCurrentEntry`), which the state now holds
  where the set-aside of 1.39 and 1.44 used to.
- A stored revision taking the current place after a later one set it aside
  fails `aRevisionSetAsideStaysSoWhenStoredLate` and
  `theChainHoldsBeforeTheNamedSaveRuns`.
- An earlier revision still waiting left out of the set-aside fails those two
  and `setsAsideOnlyTheEarlierEntriesOfTheChain`.
- A stored revision never made current fails four of the five
  `DraftRecipientsChainIT` tests, and in `DraftPersistenceServiceTest`
  `aSaveSetsAsideTheEarlierRevisionsWithoutTheirRow` and
  `saveRecoveryDraftParksContent`, which now asserts that a stored recovery
  draft becomes current: one of the two gaps the pass over 1.44 found in the
  committed tests. The other, a recovery draft whose APPEND is refused,
  `saveRecoveryDraftReturnsNullWhenAppendFails` now asserts takes no current
  place.

**Residual.** Entries that are not current are bounded together, so a save
still waiting when a thousand newer such entries are written after it loses
its entry before its revision is stored: a hold that outlasts some thousand
autosaves leaves the oldest held revisions, once stored, with no entry, and
an untouched send of one is checked against the row. Each of those is a
revision the user's later saves replaced, so this is the standing residual of
a revision the server keeps after it was replaced, reached by the same
thousand saves; the draft's current revision is not among them. An exit with
saves waiting leaves their entries accepted, outside the current count, so
the 1.46 objection to the 1.44 residual no longer applies. A save whose chain
lookup misses still starts a chain of its own (1.42).

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.47** (a separate agent's; read from the code
at `f9c4490` and measured with the IMAP side stubbed and the production
executor, pragmas and pool real, Core i7-1255U). `insertEntry` writes no
`stored_at`, and the one statement that sets it is `markStored`, which
`appendDraftMessage` reaches only after an APPEND whose outcome is
`appended` (read). A throwaway probe took every way out of a save that
stores nothing — the replaced row's lookup, the account, the message build
by an exception and by an `Error`, an APPEND answered false, one that throws
an exception and one that throws an `Error`, a false APPEND whose
`last_error` write then throws, and a task that never runs — and each left
the save's entry accepted and the draft's previous revision its one current
entry, with no call into `DraftRecipientsRepository` from the task at all. A
save waiting on the held lane is accepted while it waits, and current, with
the previous revision set aside, once released and stored. After a stored
APPEND an `Error` from the local upsert leaves the stored revision current
and the replaced one set aside and still on the server, since the delete
never runs, and a `last_error` clear that throws leaves it current.

`markStored`, run against a fixture holding every state, changed exactly
three rows: `stored_at` on the stored revision's entry, and `superseded_at`
on an earlier current entry and an earlier accepted one of its chain. It
left alone the earlier entries already set aside, stored or not, with their
times; the chain's later accepted, current and set-aside entries; another
chain's; and another account's under the same chain id. A revision with no
entry, or another account's Message-ID, changed nothing, and a revision a
later stored one had set aside, stored late, changed nothing and stayed set
aside. Through the production executor, with a fair lock for the lane and up
to 3 ms of jitter before it, six runs of 240 saves over six drafts, each
naming the one before, with outcomes drawn at random (70 % stored, 20 %
refused, 10 % throwing) and acceptances interleaved with the tasks: 1 to 15
revisions per run were stored after a later revision of their draft, a
sampler polling throughout never saw two current entries in one chain, and
every draft ended with its newest stored revision as its one current entry.

On a fixture, `deleteCurrentButNewest` counted only the entries stored and
not set aside, and `deleteNotCurrentButNewest` all the others — set aside,
stored or not, and accepted — each newest by `saved_seq`, and neither
touched another account. With the service's own sizes, an acceptance over
1,005 entries of each kind left 999 current and 1,001 others, the new
accepted entry among them. A stored recovery draft became current in a
chain named by its own stableId; a refused one returned no pointer and
changed no current entry, nor did one whose build failed; and one whose
entry could not be written was stored with none, nothing made current.

Against the 1.44 code (`f9c4490^`), with the 1.47 `DraftRecipientsChainIT`
reading "current" as 1.44 did, not set aside,
`savesWaitingForTheLaneTakeNoCurrentPlace` fails at its first assertion,
the hidden draft's entry deleted, and the other four tests pass, run and
seen. Each neutralization 1.47 lists was put in and run against
`DraftRecipientsChainIT`, `DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` and
`DraftPersistenceServiceTest`, and the code restored after each: an entry
written as stored fails exactly the eight tests 1.47 counts, five in
`DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` and three in `DraftRecipientsChainIT`, among
them the three of the 1.37, 1.39 and 1.43 routes; a set-aside revision
allowed to retake the current place fails the two it names; a waiting
revision left out of the set-aside fails the three; and the `markStored`
call made unreachable fails four of the five `DraftRecipientsChainIT` tests
and the two `DraftPersistenceServiceTest` cases. Three more, not in that
list, each fail a test as well: the current bound counting accepted entries
again fails `savesWaitingForTheLaneTakeNoCurrentPlace`,
`rejectedSavesDoNotPushOutAnotherDraft` and `theTwoKindsAreBoundedApart`;
the other bound leaving accepted entries out fails
`theTwoKindsAreBoundedApart`; and the current entries pruned to
`KEPT_DRAFT_RECIPIENTS` rather than one fewer fails
`keepingRecipientsKeepsTheNewestEntries`, which pins the argument.

The send looks an entry up by Message-ID in every state: through the real
`SmtpMessageService.sendDraftAsync` over the same database, an untouched
send whose server copy adds a Bcc is refused for an accepted, a current and
a set-aside entry, and goes out for a revision with none. The two residuals
of 1.47 hold as far as one draft goes. With 1,100 saves of one draft
accepted while the lane was held, all stored once released, the oldest 99
had no entry and the last was the draft's one current entry. An exit with
twenty saves waiting left all twenty accepted and one entry current, both
when nothing ran and when the executor was closed — the eight holding a
permit ran and failed when interrupted, the other twelve never ran.

**Reopened at 1.48** (Medium, from the same pass; measured). The 1.47
residual says the revisions a long hold leaves without an entry are each
one the user's later saves replaced, and that the draft's current revision
is not among them. That holds for one draft. The bound for entries that are
not current is the account's, not the draft's, and a draft's newest
revision is not current while its save waits for the lane, when the server
answers its APPEND NO but keeps the revision, or when `markStored` fails
after a stored APPEND. Some thousand later saves of any other draft then
push its entry out, and the draft keeps none: the newest revision's own save
deletes the revision it replaces together with that revision's entry when
the server gave it a row, and a server that did not can drop that copy
itself. An untouched send of the draft is then checked against the row,
which the server writes by withholding APPENDUID — the case the 1.20 fix was
written to close. Measured with the probe above, each route ending in the
real `sendDraftAsync`:

- **A save held while another draft autosaves.** Draft X stored, then X's
  last save accepted while the lane was held, then saves of draft Y accepted
  during the same hold. X's entry was deleted at Y's 1,001st acceptance; with
  999 it survives, and becomes current once stored. Released, X's revision
  was stored with no entry, and its save deleted X's previous revision from
  the server, and that revision's entry with it in one of three runs; in the
  other two the forget failed beside the concurrent writers (below), leaving
  a current entry for a revision no longer on the server. Nothing was
  recorded. Presented with an added Bcc under a row the sync wrote, the
  untouched send of X went out, where with 999 saves of Y it was refused. This is the attack of 1.46 with another victim, and 1.47
  did not close it: against the 1.44 code the same probe loses X's entry at
  Y's 1,000th acceptance. It costs what 1.46 cost, a hold for as long as the
  user takes to autosave some thousand times, and asks besides only that the
  user leave one draft and write another meanwhile, which is how the app
  composes: one composer at a time (`composeSession` in
  `frontend/src/lib/compose/session.ts`), the 202 returned before the
  APPEND, so leaving a draft does not wait for its save.
- **A save answered NO though stored.** X's last save's APPEND answered NO
  with the revision kept, then saves of Y that the server stores without
  APPENDUID, so each replaced revision of Y stays as a set-aside entry: X's
  entry was deleted at Y's 1,002nd acceptance, and with X's previous copy
  dropped and X presented with an added Bcc, the untouched send went out. No
  hold; one `DRAFT_SAVE_FAILED` for X's save is the visible trace. Not new at
  1.47: since 1.39 a refused save's entry is set aside and bounded with the
  superseded ones, and against the 1.44 code the same probe loses it at the
  same acceptance. 1.39 wrote that a server which stored such a revision
  after all "still has it checked against what was typed"; that lasts until
  a thousand newer entries push it out, and no residual names it, since the
  residuals name revisions a later save replaced.
- **A failed `markStored`.** It is best-effort and catches every `Exception`.
  Failed by an injected exception, and by a real `SQLITE_BUSY` with another
  connection holding the write lock for 8 s from the APPEND's answer, it
  leaves the stored revision accepted while the save goes on to delete the
  previous revision; with the injected failure that revision's entry went
  too, so the draft had no current entry, and Y's 1,002nd later save pushed
  X's entry out, after which the untouched send went out. Under 1.44 the same
  failure left the new revision current from its acceptance. No way was found
  for a server to cause it: the account's sync and the APPEND share the
  BACKGROUND lane, and `markStored` runs once the APPEND has released it
  (read).

One more observation from the same runs, not a route around the bound:
`forgetTypedRecipients` drops an entry through `deleteById`, a read and then
a write in one transaction, the shape 1.41 found for the entry's write.
Beside another connection that holds the write lock for 300 ms it fails at
once, whether that connection then commits (93 ms) or rolls back (3 ms), and
the entry stays, where a plain `DELETE` in the same spot waits and goes
through (measured). It fails toward keeping the check: a dropped revision's
entry stays, and after a send a current one stays current, so what it costs
is a current place for each send whose forget fails — by sends, not by
saves (read for the send).

Severity stays **Medium**, the finding's own, as at every reopening.
**Recommendation:** keep each draft's newest entry out of the bound that
saves fill, whatever its state — count it with the current entries, which
grow by drafts — so that the entries saves can push out are only revisions a
later save of the same draft has followed; a draft then holds at most two
such entries, its current one and its newest. Bounding how many saves an
account may have waiting closes only the first route; which, at fix time.

**Fix (1.50, 2026-10-06, #651).** As recommended. An account's entries are
still bounded in two kinds, each to its newest 1,000 by `saved_seq` at every
acceptance, but the kinds are drawn differently. **Heads** are the entry a
draft is current at and each draft's newest, whatever its state — accepted
and waiting for the lane, answered NO but kept, or left accepted by a
`markStored` that failed — so a draft holds two at most, and they grow by
drafts (`DraftRecipientsRepository.deleteHeadsButNewest`). **Followed**
entries are the rest: revisions a later save of their own draft has
followed and that are not current, set aside or never stored
(`deleteFollowedButNewest`). A draft's newest is the entry of its chain with
the largest `saved_seq`, found by one grouped subquery for the whole
statement; a per-row `NOT EXISTS` would scan a draft's chain for each of its
entries, quadratic on a draft with a thousand set-aside revisions, at every
acceptance. All three routes of 1.48 leave the draft's last revision its
newest, so a head, and another draft's autosaves now push out only that
draft's own followed entries. The heads are pruned before each insert to
one fewer than the bound, the place the new entry takes as its draft's
newest; the revision it follows stops being a head unless it is current.
Unchanged: the INSERT, the chain lookup, `markStored`, the send's lookup by
Message-ID in every state, and the bound's size. A draft whose last save is
not stored now costs two of the thousand heads rather than one, and a head
is pushed out only by newer heads, so by some five hundred other drafts at
the least. `V1__init.sql` changes in its schema comment only, with
`FlywayBaselineChecksumTest` re-pinned, as the pre-release baseline allows.
And the observation of 1.48 is taken up: `forgetTypedRecipients` drops an
entry with a plain DELETE (`deleteEntry`), which waits for SQLite's write
lock, rather than `deleteById`, whose read and then write failed at once
beside another connection's write and left the entry.

**Dynamic verification (1.50).** Through the service over SQLite with the
production pragmas and pool, `DraftRecipientsChainIT` holds a draft's last
save accepted, as while a server holds the lane, then makes 1,002 stored
autosaves of another draft on a server that withholds APPENDUID: the held
revision's entry stays, and once released it is the draft's current one
(`aHeldLastSaveOutlastsAnotherDraftsAutosaves`). With the draft's last save
answered NO instead, its entry and its stored revision's both stay
(`aLastSaveAnsweredNoOutlastsAnotherDraftsAutosaves`). The third route, a
failed `markStored`, leaves the same state, accepted and newest, which
`DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT.aDraftsNewestEntryOutlastsAnotherDraftsAutosaves`
covers on a fixture. All three fail with the 1.47 statements put back into
the two methods, as does the rewritten `theTwoKindsAreBoundedApart`, run and
seen; the rest of both suites passes either way. The repository fixture's
bound goes from three to four, since a draft whose save the server refuses
now holds two heads. `forgettingWaitsOutAConcurrentWriter` forgets an entry
while another connection holds the write lock and then commits: the forget
waits and the entry goes, and with `deleteById` put back it returns at once
with the entry in place, run and seen.

**Residual.** A revision a later save of its draft has followed, and that
is not current, can still be pushed out by some thousand later saves: the
residual of 1.47, which 1.48 found held for one draft only, now holds across
drafts, since every revision the bound can push out is one the user's later
saves of the same draft replaced.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.50** (1.53; a separate session's, read from
the code at `4e1b8d7` and measured with the IMAP side stubbed and the
production pragmas and pool real, as `DraftRecipientsChainIT` runs). The two
statements read as 1.50 describes them. A head is an entry stored and not
set aside, or the entry with the largest `saved_seq` of its chain; a
followed entry is any other, so the two kinds split an account's entries
exactly, and since `saved_seq` is `NOT NULL` the `NOT IN` over the
grouped `MAX` cannot be emptied by a NULL. A chain holds two heads at most:
`markStored` sets `stored_at` only on an entry not set aside and in the same
statement sets aside every earlier entry of the chain, so a chain has one
current entry at most, and its newest is the other. New chains come only
from what the user does — a save naming nothing (a new compose), one naming
a stableId with no entry here (the first save here of a draft composed
elsewhere or presented under a stableId of the server's, or a compose moved
to another account), and a failed send's recovery draft. The client's one
caller of the save, `saveOnce` in `frontend/src/lib/compose/session.ts`,
names the stableId the previous 202 returned and keeps it when a save fails,
so no answer of the server's starts a chain (read). The heads do count every
chain the user leaves behind, sent ones among them: a send forgets only the
sent revision's entry, and only once the server lets the client find and
delete that revision, so on a server that refuses the delete or withholds
APPENDUID each composed message keeps one or two heads. The "thousand drafts
of the user's own work" of 1.29 is a thousand composed messages, as
`KEPT_DRAFT_RECIPIENTS` says.

With the 1.47 statements put back into the two methods, exactly the four
tests 1.50 names fail — `aHeldLastSaveOutlastsAnotherDraftsAutosaves`,
`aLastSaveAnsweredNoOutlastsAnotherDraftsAutosaves`,
`aDraftsNewestEntryOutlastsAnotherDraftsAutosaves` and
`theTwoKindsAreBoundedApart` — and the rest of both suites and
`DraftPersistenceServiceTest` pass, run and seen. Each half put back alone:
the followed bound counting each chain's newest again fails the same four;
the heads leaving the newest out fails only `theTwoKindsAreBoundedApart`,
since an accepted newest is then pruned by neither bound and outlasts the
autosaves all the same, and what goes is only its bound, one per chain.
`deleteById` put back into `forgetTypedRecipients` fails
`forgettingWaitsOutAConcurrentWriter` and the two
`DraftPersistenceServiceTest` cases that pin the call, run and seen.

**Reopened at 1.53** (Medium, from the same pass; measured). The residual of
1.50 is the attack of 1.48 one revision back. Every revision a later save of
its draft has followed shares the account's followed bound with every other
draft's saves, so the revision before a draft's newest is pushed out by some
thousand autosaves of another draft, as the newest was until 1.50. It asks
for nothing the user can see. A server that withholds APPENDUID, as one
without UIDPLUS does honestly, gives the replaced revision no row, so the
save that replaced it cannot find it to delete it and the server keeps it;
one that refuses the delete keeps it the same way. Measured through the
service with a throwaway IT on `DraftRecipientsChainIT`'s setup: draft X
saved three times and stored each time without APPENDUID, then 1,100 stored
autosaves of draft Y. X's second revision lost its entry at Y's 1,002nd
save, the count of 1.48's routes, and X's third, its current one, kept its
entry. The server then hides the newest revision and presents the one before
it with a Bcc added: it has no entry, so its untouched send is checked
against the row the sync wrote from the server's copy and goes out (read;
the end of the chain 1.48 measured for the newest). The user sees the draft without
its last autosave — what was typed before the last pause — with no
`DRAFT_SAVE_FAILED`, no hold and nothing recorded. The residual's reason,
that each such revision is "one the user's later saves of the same draft
replaced", does not set it apart from the draft the user means to send: the
server chooses which revision to present, and the one before the newest
costs it no more to reach. Severity stays **Medium**, the finding's own.
**Recommendation:** bound the followed entries by draft rather than by
account, so that no other draft's saves can push out a revision of this one
— for instance, keep every followed entry of a chain that still has a head,
and drop a chain's followed entries with its last head. They then grow by
the saves of the drafts the heads keep, all the user's own, and only while
the server keeps the revisions they replaced; nothing the server answers
adds one. If that is too much, keep each chain's newest few dozen followed
entries and bound the rest by account, so that the revision a server can
reach is some dozens of saves older than the newest, which the user would
see is not the draft. Which, at fix time.

**Fix (1.54, 2026-10-07, #655).** As recommended, by the first way. An
account keeps its typed recipients by draft. Its newest `KEPT_DRAFTS`
(1,000) drafts, each a chain as old as its newest entry, are kept whole,
every revision of them, and an older draft goes whole
(`DraftRecipientsRepository.deleteDraftsButNewest`). Only a save that starts
a draft — its chain is its own stableId — adds one, so only such a save
prunes the drafts, keeping the one it starts besides the newest 999; a save
of a draft already kept skips that statement. No save of one draft can push
out a revision of another, whatever the server answers, and since only the
user starts a draft, what pushes one out is a thousand messages composed
since, sent ones included when the server refuses their clean-up. Within a
draft, the revisions a later save of it followed and that are not current
keep their entries up to its newest `KEPT_REVISIONS_PER_DRAFT` (100), pruned
at each of that draft's saves to one fewer (`deleteRevisionsButNewest`); the
draft's current entry and its newest are never among them. The revision a
server can present without its entry is therefore a hundred of the draft's
own autosaves behind the newest. The heads and followed kinds of 1.50 are
gone. `chain_id` is `NOT NULL` in `V1__init.sql`, as every entry has carried
one since 1.40 (a NULL among them would have emptied the `NOT IN` of the
drafts bound), and the chain index carries `saved_seq`, so a draft's age is
read from the index; `FlywayBaselineChecksumTest` is re-pinned, so a dev
database needs a reset. Cost, measured at the worst the bounds allow (1,000
drafts of 101 entries over the production pragmas): some 83 ms to accept a
save that starts a draft and some 12 ms for a save of a draft already kept,
against some 90 ms for every save with the drafts statement run each time.

**Dynamic verification (1.54).**
`DraftRecipientsChainIT.aDraftsReplacedRevisionsOutlastAnotherDraftsAutosaves`
saves a draft three times, each stored without APPENDUID, then makes 1,002
stored autosaves of another draft: both replaced revisions keep their
entries. With the 1.50 statements put back, and the followed bound at a
thousand as 1.50 had it, that test fails, and so does
`aDraftsOwnSavesBoundItsReplacedRevisions`, which checks that 120 saves of
one draft leave its newest hundred replaced revisions and its current entry;
the other eight pass, the routes of 1.48 among them, run and seen.
`DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` covers the same route on a fixture of three
drafts and three revisions (`aDraftsReplacedRevisionsOutlastAnotherDraftsAutosaves`),
a draft going whole while the one being saved stays however old
(`aDraftGoesWhole`), and a draft's revisions bounded by its own saves alone
(`aDraftsRevisionsAreBoundedByItsOwnSaves`). With the revision bound taken
back to the account, the first and the last of these fail, and the
service-level test with them; with the exemption of the draft being saved
removed, `aDraftGoesWhole` fails; both run and seen. `DraftPersistenceServiceTest`
pins both bounds' arguments, and that a save of a draft already kept prunes
no draft.

**Residual.** A revision a hundred later saves of its own draft have
followed, and that is not current, goes, and a server keeping it can present
it without its entry: a draft a hundred autosaves behind the one the user
last saw. A draft goes with a thousand newer drafts of the user's, sent ones
among them, whatever its state. The earlier residuals stand: a draft the
server presents under a Message-ID of its own is checked like one composed
elsewhere, and a save whose chain lookup misses starts a draft of its own.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.54** (1.57; a separate agent's, read from the
code at `05e8ca4` and measured with the IMAP and SMTP sides stubbed and the
production pragmas and pool real). The bounds work as built:
`deleteDraftsButNewest` keeps the newest 999 other drafts and the one being
saved, each whole, a draft as old as its newest `saved_seq`, and runs only
for a save whose chain is its own stableId; `deleteRevisionsButNewest`
touches only followed entries, never a draft's newest or its current one, 99
before the insert and so at most 100 after; `chain_id` is `NOT NULL`, with the
entity and the index to match. A server cannot make one draft's saves count
as many: a chain starts only from a save naming nothing, one naming a
stableId with no entry, or a recovery draft, and the client names the
stableId the previous 202 returned, saves only changed content and retries
nothing by itself (read). Two acceptances that each start a draft, in flight
together, can leave 1,001 drafts until the next one prunes, and a save that
looked up its chain just before a concurrent new draft pruned it writes it
back with one entry; both only through the user's own saves (read). Each
neutralization 1.54 lists fails the tests it names, run and seen, and two
the pass added fail as well: the drafts statement run on every save fails
`anAcceptedSaveJoinsTheNamedRevisionsChain`, and the per-draft argument at
100 rather than 99 fails it, `keepingRecipientsKeepsTheNewestEntries` and
`aDraftsOwnSavesBoundItsReplacedRevisions`.

**Reopened at 1.57** (Medium, from the same pass; measured). 1.54's residual
holds for the bounds and not for the save. A save that replaces a revision
deletes it from the server and then forgets its entry whatever the server
answered: `saveDraftAsync` ignores what `ImapActionService.hardDelete`
returns — false for a refused STORE or EXPUNGE — and goes on to
`forgetTypedRecipients`, and a server that answers OK and keeps the message
looks the same to the client as one that deleted it. So with APPENDUID, which
gives the replaced revision a row to delete, the server keeps revision 1
after revision 2's save, the client forgets revision 1's entry, and the
server hides revision 2 and presents revision 1 with a Bcc added: its
untouched send is checked against the row the sync wrote and goes out.
Measured with a throwaway IT on `DraftRecipientsChainIT`'s setup, through the
real `DraftPersistenceService` and `SmtpMessageService.sendDraftAsync`: with
the delete refused, and with it claimed while the message was kept, the
entry was gone and the send went out; with no row to delete, the entry
stayed and the send was refused. It costs one autosave, no hold and nothing
from another draft, and an honest server's transient NO reaches the same
state. Not new at 1.54 — the forget after the delete is 1.20's — but it is
the route 1.53 and 1.54 were written to close, by one save rather than a
thousand or a hundred. The send paths forget the same way after their delete
(`SmtpMessageService.sendDraftAsync`, `deleteSupersededDraft`). Severity
stays **Medium**, the finding's own. **Recommendation:** do not forget a
replaced revision's entry for anything the server answers to its delete:
`markStored` has set it aside already, and the per-draft and per-account
bounds cover it. On the send paths, set the sent revision's entry aside
rather than delete it. Checking `hardDelete`'s result does not do, since a
server can claim the delete and keep the message. A `DraftRecipientsChainIT`
case with the delete refused, and one with it claimed, should each keep the
entry.

**Corrections (1.57).** 1.54's Fix and Residual and the javadocs of
`KEPT_REVISIONS_PER_DRAFT` and `deleteRevisionsButNewest` say the revision a
server can present without its entry is a hundred saves behind the newest; by
the route above it is one. 1.53 says a server that "refuses the delete keeps
it the same way": it keeps the revision, and the client does not keep the
entry. 1.36 says that where the server deletes the replaced revision its
entry is dropped at once: it is dropped whether or not the server deleted
it. 1.20 says the 40 MiB bound on the fetched draft is "the only thing
between a hostile server and as much heap as it cares to spend": the header
block the send parses from it keeps some 26 times its size (B1-18, §4r).

**Fix (1.58, 2026-10-07, #659).** As recommended. A save that replaces a
revision no longer forgets its entry: `saveDraftAsync` still deletes the
revision from the server and its row, and the entry stays, set aside by the
append's `markStored`, whatever the server answers to the delete. The send
paths set the sent revision's entry aside rather than drop it, after
`sendDraftAsync`'s delete and in `deleteSupersededDraft` alike
(`DraftPersistenceService.setAsideTypedRecipients`, through
`DraftRecipientsRepository.setAside`, a plain UPDATE of `superseded_at` that
waits for SQLite's write lock as the DELETE did). `forgetTypedRecipients`
and `deleteEntry` are gone: no path of this client drops an entry on its own
any more, and entries go only by the two bounds of 1.54. Cost: a replaced
revision's entry now stays on every server, honest ones included, so the
user's own autosaves reach those bounds where before only a server that kept
the replaced revisions did, and the worst 1.54 measured, 1,000 drafts of 101
entries, is reachable by ordinary use, at the costs measured then. The
comment in `V1__init.sql` says so; `FlywayBaselineChecksumTest` is
re-pinned, so a dev database needs a reset.

**Dynamic verification (1.58).**
`DraftRecipientsChainIT.aReplacedRevisionKeepsItsEntryWhateverTheDeleteAnswers`
gives a draft's first revision the row a save with APPENDUID writes, so the
next save addresses it and deletes it, and runs once with the server
refusing the delete and once with it claiming the delete: in both the
replaced revision keeps its entry and the newest is the one current.
`aSupersededDraftKeepsItsEntryWhenTheDeleteIsClaimed` does the same through
`deleteSupersededDraft`, and `settingAsideWaitsOutAConcurrentWriter`, which
replaces the forget's lock test of 1.48, has the update wait out another
connection's write and leave the entry, set aside.
`DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT.aSentDraftsEntryIsSetAsideAndKept` pins the
statement: the named entry only, not another account's, and an entry set
aside already keeps its time. With the 1.57 forget put back into the save,
both runs of the first test fail and the other eleven pass; with the send's
set-aside turned back into the DELETE, the second and the third fail and the
save's pass; `DraftPersistenceServiceTest.appendSuccessDeletesOldDraft` and
`deleteSupersededHardDeletesOwnDraft` fail with the respective one; all run
and seen. `SmtpMessageServiceTest` pins that `sendDraftAsync` sets the
entry aside.

**Residual (1.58).** 1.54's residual holds again, for the delete as well: a
revision a hundred later saves of its own draft have followed, and that is
not current, goes, and a server keeping it can present it without its entry,
whatever it answered to the deletes. A sent draft's entries go with a
thousand newer drafts. The earlier residuals stand.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.58** (1.64; a separate agent's, read from the
code at `f789efd2` and run against it). The save deletes the replaced
revision and its row and keeps its entry; `markStored` has set it aside by
then; both send paths set the sent revision's entry aside with
`DraftRecipientsRepository.setAside`, an UPDATE limited to entries not set
aside yet. No path of this client drops an entry on its own: the two bounds
of 1.54 are the only DELETE statements, and otherwise only the account's
`ON DELETE CASCADE` removes entries (every use of the repository grepped).
The check reads the entry for the row's Message-ID whatever its state, so an
entry set aside still checks. Each neutralization 1.58 lists fails the tests
it names, run and seen, and two more were pinned: without
`superseded_at IS NULL`, `aSentDraftsEntryIsSetAsideAndKept` fails, and
without the call in `sendDraftAsync`,
`SmtpMessageServiceTest.draftSavedHereMatchingTheTypedRecipientsSends`
fails. The send's set-aside put back as a DELETE in the SQL rather than as
the old call passes the mock test, which pins the call, not the statement;
the repository IT pins the statement. No route around was found: a supersede
that finds no row leaves the entry current, which still checks; a re-presented
sent revision finds its entry; B1-18's `UntouchedDraft` gives the check and
the SMTP envelope the same parsed top-level headers; newsgroups are refused by
`SMTPTransport` and a group survives normalization whole, so it mismatches
unless the user typed it. The residual of 1.58 holds as written.

**Status: fixed at 1.58**, confirmed by the pass over 1.58 (1.64). Fixed at 1.14, 1.20, 1.29, 1.36, 1.39, 1.40,
1.42, 1.44, 1.47, 1.50, 1.54 and 1.58; reopened at 1.16, when the recipient check held for a
draft composed here only when the server returned APPENDUID, at 1.24, for
the key and the age limit, at 1.31, for what the count counts, at 1.41, for
the order in which a save finds its chain and for the write of its entry, at
1.43, for a save whose task fails before its APPEND, at 1.46, for the saves
a server keeps waiting, each current from its acceptance, at 1.48, for a
draft's newest revision, bounded with every other draft's saves while it is
not current, at 1.53, for the revision before it, which the followed bound still left to every other draft's saves, and at 1.57, for the entry a save forgot whatever the server answered to its delete; the passes over 1.37 and
1.39 each found a route around the count, closed at 1.39 and 1.40. The
passes over 1.44, 1.47, 1.50, 1.54 and 1.58 confirmed those fixes. The residuals above stand as well: a draft the server presents under a Message-ID of its own is
checked like one composed elsewhere.

## 4f. Finding B1-6 (Low) — no IMAP write timeout — **FIXED**

**What.** The IMAP stores set `mail.<proto>.timeout` (read) and
`.connectiontimeout` but no `.writetimeout` (`ImapConnectionManager`,
`MailConnectionProbe`); SMTP sets all three. A server that stops reading — a
full TCP window during an APPEND, a stalled command — blocks the writing
thread indefinitely, and with it that lane's lock for the account.

**Severity: Low**, the same threat row as the slow-server hang (B1-D):
recoverable by restart, confined to one account's lane, and a hostile server
can stall more cheaply by not answering, which the read timeout covers.

**Recommendation.** Set `mail.<proto>.writetimeout` from a new
`mail.client.imap.write-timeout`. Angus implements it with a scheduler per
connection, a cost to weigh against the lane count.

**Fix (shipped 2026-09-23).** The recommendation, with the cost it named not
paid. `ImapSocketTimeouts` now sets all three bounds for the pool and the
probe alike — the third helper of that shape, beside `ImapTransportSecurity`,
and for the same reason: the two paths cannot disagree about a session's
settings if only one place writes them. The value comes from a new
`mail.client.imap.write-timeout`, 60 s, the read timeout's number.

It can sit that high because of what it bounds: one `write` call, not a
command. Angus schedules a task per write and cancels it on return, so what has
to finish inside the budget is a socket buffer draining, not an APPEND — a
large message on a slow link never runs into it, while a server that has
stopped reading hits it on the first write past the window.

The scheduler is shared rather than left to Angus, which otherwise builds a
`ScheduledThreadPoolExecutor` per connection: an account holds two IMAP
connections and each open folder another, and `SmtpTransportFactory` opens a
transport per message sent, so the default would have cost a platform thread
apiece. The session names one instead, under
`mail.<proto>.executor.writetimeout`, which Angus reads as an object rather
than as a string; `MailWriteTimeoutScheduler` holds it, one daemon thread that
times out when idle, and SMTP now names it too. It is deliberately never shut
down: a socket outlives the bean that opened it during shutdown, and scheduling
on a stopped executor would fail the write the timeout exists to protect.

Verified over a socket, not by reading the property back, because a key Angus
does not recognize leaves the connection as unbounded as it was with nothing to
say so: `ImapSocketTimeoutsTest` writes to a server that accepts the
connection and then reads nothing from it. With the bound the write fails in
about a second; with it removed the write never returns and the test ends
thirty seconds later on its own timeout, which is the finding.

**Status: fixed**, in the same version as this note.

## 4g. Finding B1-7 (Medium) — a declared literal size is allocated before a byte arrives — **FIXED**

**What.** An IMAP literal announces its length (`{n}` at the end of a
line), and Angus's `ResponseInputStream.readResponse` grows its buffer to
`n` before reading the literal's bytes. `n` is the server's. Measured
against Angus 2.0.5 on a 384 MB heap (at 1.11, through
`IMAPProtocol.readResponse` over a stream): a declared `{2000000000}` fails
at once with `OutOfMemoryError`, and a declared `{300000000}` followed by
three bytes takes about 300 MB of heap before the stream ends. On a socket the
server simply stops sending, and the buffer stays allocated until the read
timeout — long enough for the rest of the process to run out. Any response can
carry a literal, the ones the sync fetches included, so it needs no user
action. The allocation happens before the response exists as an object, so
`BoundedImapProtocol` (§4c) cannot see it; and B1-1's 8 MiB body cap (§4)
bounds the bytes our code reads, not the buffer a partial fetch's literal
declares.

**Severity: Medium** (_DoS recoverable by restart_), the same precondition as
B1-3: a hostile or compromised server, automatic sync.

**Recommendation.** Refuse a declared size above a bound before Angus reads
it, which means sitting under `ResponseInputStream` — a stream wrapper on the
connection's socket, or `ByteArray.grow` in a buffer the protocol hands
Angus. Which, and the bound (a literal legitimately carries a whole message
when partial fetch is off), is a design decision at fix time.

**Fix (shipped 2026-09-22).** The second of the two, because the first is not
reachable: `Protocol` builds its own socket and keeps the stream in a private
field, so no wrapper can be put under `ResponseInputStream` without reflection,
while `getResponseBuffer()` is `protected` and `ByteArray.grow` is public and
non-final. `BoundedImapProtocol` therefore hands Angus a `BoundedByteArray`,
which refuses to grow past `MAX_RESPONSE_BYTES` = **32 MiB**. Reading the
bytecode of Angus 2.0.5 pins why that is enough of a hook: `readResponse` takes
every byte of a response through `ba.grow`, the buffer's doubling for a long
line included, and `Response` reads only `getBytes()` and `getCount()` back off
it — so wrapping the caller's buffer keeps its backing array in use and changes
nothing else. The override must return non-null, because `readResponse` makes a
plain `ByteArray` of its own for a null, which no bound would reach.

32 MiB is set the way B1-3's bounds are: far above what this client asks for,
far below what hurts it. `partialfetch` is pinned on (§1) and Angus's
`fetchsize` default is 16 KiB, so a body literal is three orders of magnitude
under the bound however heavy the message; the headroom is for a server that
ignores the partial request, and above it `MimePartExtractor`'s 8 MiB cap (§4)
would have served the placeholder anyway.

The refusal reaches the connection the same way B1-3's does. `grow` cannot
declare a checked exception, so it throws an unchecked one and
`readResponse()` turns it into the `IOException` that Angus ends the command
with. It catches every `RuntimeException` there, not only that one, and the
reason is the two shapes a literal takes at the top of the `int` range, both
measured on 2.0.5: a declared size in 2147483603..2147483631 overflows the
array length inside `grow` (`NegativeArraySizeException`), and 2147483632 and
up overflow Angus's own "does it fit" test, so it skips the grow and reads past
the buffer (`IndexOutOfBoundsException`). The first shape does reach `grow`,
and the bounded buffer refuses it there before the overflow can happen
(`BoundedImapProtocolTest.overflowingIncrementIsRefused`); only the second
bypasses the bound, since it allocates nothing (1.13–1.15 said neither shape
reaches it). An unchecked exception crossing `readResponse`
leaves the connection mid-response in the pool, where the next command would
read the rest of this one as its own reply, since `ImapFolderExecutor` turns a
`RuntimeException` into a `MailOperationException` and closes only the folder.
A genuine parser fault is handled the same way on purpose: the connection's
state is unknown either way, and the cause is kept for the log. That holds
for a fault that is a `RuntimeException`. One that is an `Error` is not
caught; the one Angus's `BODYSTRUCTURE` parser raised is prevented before the
parse since B1-10's fix (§4j; 1.13–1.15 said every parser fault), and since 1.32 for a hostile server too, which hid the nesting from the earlier checks.

**Dynamic verification.** `HostileImapResponseIT` gains both shapes over the
wire — a literal one byte past the bound, and one declared at
`Integer.MAX_VALUE`. Against the unfixed code the first ends the pass in
`SocketTimeoutException: Read timed out` about ten seconds later, having
allocated the 32 MiB first; it was run that way and seen to fail.
`BoundedImapProtocolTest` pins the bound at its edge, that a grown buffer keeps
its bytes, and that an increment which would overflow the array length is
refused rather than attempted.

**Residual.** A single IMAP response above 32 MiB cannot be read. Nothing the
client asks for reaches that with partial fetch on; a server that ignores it
and serves a part larger than 32 MiB in one literal ends the connection instead
of the message being skipped.

**Status: fixed.**

## 4h. Finding B1-8 (Medium) — every bound is per response, and a command's responses add up — **FIXED**

**What.** Angus's `Protocol.command` collects every response a command
receives into one list and hands the list back when the tagged completion
arrives; the caller acts on the responses only then (read from the 2.0.5
bytecode). B1-3's checks and B1-7's buffer each hold **one** response to a
bound, so what a command holds at once is bounded by nothing. Two measurements
at 1.16:

- **Hostile**, by the verification pass. A server that advertises QRESYNC
  answers a QRESYNC SELECT with 60 lines of `* VANISHED (EARLIER) 1:1000000`
  (about 2 KB) under a raised UIDNEXT. Each line is inside the 1,000,000 bound.
  `IMAPFolder.open` expands each into its own `long[]` and
