  `MessageVanishedEvent`, and keeps all of them until it returns: 60,000,000
  UIDs, **499 MB** retained, against the packaged 384 MB heap. The
  preconditions are the server's to meet — it advertises QRESYNC, and the
  folder has a MODSEQ baseline and a local UID range narrower than 1,000,000
  (`MailSyncService.buildResyncRequest`) — and they are the normal state from
  a folder's second cycle on, in automatic sync. Nothing about the shape is
  particular to VANISHED: any response the bounds admit can be repeated, up to
  32 MiB at a time.
- **Honest**, by the author. The sync's UID listing, `UID FETCH 1:* (UID)`
  (`ImapCondstoreCommands.fetchAllServerUids`), gets one response per message
  in the folder, and all of them are held until the listing ends. Measured
  over TLS through `BoundedImapStore`: 500,000 messages retained **154 MB**
  (147 MiB), 308 bytes a response, before the `HashSet<Long>` built from
  them; the verification pass reproduced it at 154.8 MB. The listing runs on every cycle
  whose SELECT did not resynchronize, on a server with CONDSTORE or QRESYNC,
  because then nothing else names deletions
  (`MailSyncService.syncFlagsAndDeletions`): always on a server with CONDSTORE
  but not QRESYNC, and on a QRESYNC server whenever a folder cannot be
  resynchronized — among other cases its first cycle, a folder without a
  MODSEQ baseline, one whose local UID range spans 1,000,000 or more (§4c), and
  after a QRESYNC open that failed. After a
  resynchronizing SELECT it runs hourly, as the hole scan, and on a server with
  neither capability not at all. At the 2,000,000 the EXISTS bound admits, the
  listing alone would take about 600 MB, and up to four accounts sync at once
  (`mail.client.sync.max-concurrent-accounts`).

On the packaged JVM either shape ends in `-XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError`: the
process exits 3, the client restarts it as a recoverable stop, and the next
sync pass runs into the same folder — the crash loop §4c's residual held the
flag back for.

**Severity: Medium** (_DoS_), B1-3's: automatic sync, no user interaction, a
hostile or compromised server — or, for the listing, an honest folder large
enough. A restart does not end it: the next pass meets the same folder.

**Recommendation.** A budget per command, besides the bounds per response:
charge each response read since the last tagged one for its bytes, for a fixed
share of the objects around them, and for the UIDs a VANISHED names, and
refuse the command past the budget the way B1-3's refusals close the
connection. The client's own commands whose answers grow with the folder
cannot sit under such a budget and should stop collecting: the UID listing
can read and drop its responses one at a time instead of going through
`Protocol.command`, so what it costs is the set it builds. The same question
has to be asked of the other commands that answer per message rather than per
batch — `CHANGEDSINCE` over `1:*`, and the changed-flag FETCH responses of a
QRESYNC SELECT. The budget itself is a decision at fix time.

**Fix (shipped 2026-09-28, completed at 1.23).** Both halves of the
recommendation. `BoundedImapProtocol.readResponse` charges every untagged
response up to the tagged one that completes the command to a
`CommandBudget`: its bytes on the wire plus `RESPONSE_OVERHEAD_BYTES` = 280,
the objects around the smallest response at the 308 bytes measured at 1.16,
and the UIDs a VANISHED names. The budget is `MAX_COMMAND_BYTES` = **64 MiB**,
twice the one-response bound so that the one command that may carry a response
that large still fits, and `MAX_COMMAND_VANISHED_UIDS` = **2,000,000**, as many
UIDs as a folder may hold messages. The response that overspends either is
refused as B1-3's are: an `IOException`, a synthetic BYE, the connection
closed, the pass failed. The values are the owner's decision of 2026-09-28 to
take them from the bounds already in the class rather than from a measurement
on real accounts. The 60-line VANISHED SELECT of 1.16 now ends at its third
line. At 1.22 the charge sat in an override of `Protocol.command`, which the
author took for the one place Angus collects responses; reviewing the fix
before its verification pass, the author found that Angus's authentication
collects the responses to AUTHENTICATE in a loop of its own, until the tagged
one, so a server offering `AUTH=PLAIN` (or XOAUTH2, for the OAuth accounts)
could flood the sign-in past any budget. Charging in `readResponse` covers
both, and anything else that reads the same way.

The client's own commands that answer once per message no longer go through
`Protocol.command`. `ImapCondstoreCommands.readEach` does what that method
does in Angus 2.0.5 — read from its bytecode, step for step and in the same
order — but hands each response to the caller and to the folder's handlers and
keeps none, and tells `BoundedImapProtocol` so, which leaves those responses
uncharged; the UID listing costs the set it builds and `CHANGEDSINCE` the
list of changes. That set is bounded too, since 1.23: `readEach` hands on at
most as many responses as a folder may hold messages (`MAX_MESSAGES`), reads
and drops the rest, and fails the command once it is complete, so the
connection stays in step. At 1.22 nothing bounded it — the per-response
bounds do not count responses, and a server could answer the listing with more
distinct UIDs than the heap holds — which the author found in the same review. The other
per-message answer §4h named, the changed-flag FETCHes of a QRESYNC SELECT,
stays under the budget: a mass flag change that pushes one past it is refused,
and `ImapFolderExecutor.openForSync` opens the folder again with CONDSTORE in
the same pass, whose `CHANGEDSINCE` is read one response at a time.
Authentication is charged like any other command.

**Dynamic verification.** `HostileImapResponseIT` answers a folder open with
250,000 `* OK` lines — each passes every per-response check, three megabytes
on the wire — and the pass fails with the budget's refusal; with the budget
switched off the pass completes, which was run and seen. The same class lists
250,000 UIDs over the wire through `fetchAllServerUids` and gets every one;
sent through `Protocol.command` instead, the listing is refused at the budget
("passed 67108864 bytes"), which was run and seen. `ImapCondstoreCommandsTest`
pins `readEach`: the UIDs and flag changes it collects, that it never calls
`Protocol.command` (failing against the collecting version, run and seen), a
response that fails to parse skipped, a lost connection ending in a BYE.
`BoundedImapProtocolTest` pins the arithmetic: the byte budget, the overhead
on short responses, the VANISHED sum, and the count of either VANISHED form.
No test drives a QRESYNC server — `HostileImapServer` and GreenMail advertise
neither QRESYNC nor CONDSTORE — so the reopening after a refused QRESYNC SELECT
rests on `openForSync`'s existing handling of a failed QRESYNC open, not on a
test of this refusal. For the 1.23 half, `HostileImapResponseIT` offers
`AUTH=PLAIN` and answers AUTHENTICATE with 250,000 `* OK` lines: the pass
fails with the budget's refusal, and with the charge back in `command` alone
it completes, which was run and seen. `ImapCondstoreCommandsTest` gives
`readEach` a limit of two against three responses: it keeps two, reads the
command to its completion and fails; without the limit it keeps all three,
which was run and seen. It also checks `readEach` tells a
`BoundedImapProtocol` when a command is read one response at a time, and
when it ends.

**Residual.** The UID set and the change list still grow with the folder, up
to `MAX_MESSAGES` entries: a `HashSet<Long>` of 2,000,000 UIDs is about 100 MB,
the price of enumerating deletions rather than being told them. The budget
bounds one command, not an account: one account's three connections at the
budget together hold 192 MiB, half the packaged heap, and a second hostile
account at the same moment could still exhaust it — a window, priced here, not
the unbounded sum it was. With up to four accounts syncing at once
(`mail.client.sync.max-concurrent-accounts`), twelve connections at the budget
would hold 768 MiB, twice the heap, which the out-of-memory exit then ends. An
honest command that collects past the budget is refused: a QRESYNC SELECT after
a flag change on some 200,000 messages (opened again with CONDSTORE, one extra
connection), or an `EXPUNGE` of as many
messages flagged `\Deleted` elsewhere, which fails the delete that sent it.
The overhead is priced for the smallest response; a response Angus builds more
from, an envelope, is charged its bytes, which cover most of it.

**Reopened at 1.24** (Medium, from the verification pass). Two ways past the
fix. The budget started over at any tagged response, and `Protocol.command`
and the AUTHENTICATE loops collect a tagged response whose tag is not the
command's own and read on: a server that puts `zz OK x` among its lines
resets the budget at will, and the 1.16 flood is back. Measured:
`HostileImapResponseIT` answers a folder open with 250,000 lines, every
thousandth tagged with a foreign tag, and the pass completes. And `readEach`
hands every response to the folder's response handlers, where Angus's
`IMAPFolder` keeps a UID-table entry per distinct UID, and an `IMAPMessage`
per sequence number within EXISTS, for as long as the folder is open —
uncharged, past `readEach`'s limit, and across commands, so the budget does
not bound what one open folder accumulates (read from Angus 2.0.5's bytecode,
not measured). **Recommendation:** start the budget over only when the client
writes a command, and charge every response until then, tagged or not; hand
the streamed listing's own FETCH responses to no handler.

**Fixed again at 1.25** (2026-09-29), both halves of that recommendation and a
third the fix turned up. `BoundedImapProtocol` overrides `writeCommand` and
starts a new `CommandBudget` there — the one point every command the client
sends passes, `Protocol.command`, the AUTHENTICATE loops and `readEach`
alike — and charges every response read until the next command, tagged or
not; nothing the server sends starts a budget over. `readEach` hands an
untagged FETCH to its caller and to no response handler. No caller read what
the handler recorded: the listing's answer is the returned set, the change
list is built from the responses themselves, and a later `getMessagesByUID`
asks the server for any UID the folder does not know (`FlagSyncService`, the
one caller of both commands). The same change stops an honest listing from
making an `IMAPMessage` and a UID-table entry per message in the folder, a
cost the 1.16 measurement of the listing did not include. Only those FETCH
responses go uncharged: anything else among them — a VANISHED, an EXPUNGE, a
filler `* OK` — reaches the handlers and is charged as in any command. The
third: past its limit `readEach` read and dropped the rest for free, so a
server that never completed the listing kept the sync reading for as long as
it kept sending; what it drops is now charged, and refused once the budget is
spent.

Dynamic verification, each with the 1.23 behaviour put back and seen to fail.
`HostileImapResponseIT` answers a folder open with 250,000 lines, every
thousandth tagged with a foreign tag, and the pass fails with the budget's
refusal. It lists 100,000 UIDs, every line renaming the one message in the
folder, and the folder's UID table is empty after the listing; with the FETCH
responses handed to the handler it held 100,000 entries. It pads a listing
with 250,000 `* OK` lines, and the listing is refused at the budget.
`ImapCondstoreCommandsTest` pins the two seams: which responses reach a
handler, and that a bounded protocol charges what `readEach` drops past its
limit.

**Confirmed at 1.31**, re-derived from the code and from Angus 2.0.5's
bytecode. `writeCommand` is the only place a budget starts: it drops the
current one, and `readResponse` makes the next at the first response it
reads. Every path that sends a command reaches it — `Protocol.command`,
`authlogin`, `authplain`, `authntlm`, `authoauth2` and the SASL
authenticator all call `writeCommand`, the continuation data of an
AUTHENTICATE goes to the output stream without a new budget, and the client
sends no IDLE. `readEach` hands an untagged FETCH to no handler and every
other response to them, and the uncharged flag is cleared past its limit and
in a `finally`. The suites named above pass at `64633fe`.

**Status: fixed at 1.25**, with the residual above. What the folder keeps from
the charged FETCH responses outlives the command that brought them, and adds
up across the commands of one open folder: B1-14, §4n, bounded per open
folder since 1.49.

## 4i. Finding B1-9 (Medium) — the lazy page fetch allocates for every position down to the page — **FIXED**

**What.** A listing page below the local mirror is downloaded by the request
that asks for it (`MailFacade.getEmails` → `MailSyncService.lazyFetchPageRange`).
The range runs from the mirror's lower edge down to the requested page —
`endSeq = count − localCount`, `startSeq = count − needed + 1`, clamped to 1 —
and `MessageDownloader.downloadSequenceRange` takes it in a single
`folder.getMessages(startSeq, endSeq)` and a single `MessageFetcher.fetchBatch`.
The other download paths work in windows or batches; this one does not. The
page number is bounded only by `@Min(0)` on the two listing endpoints — the
folder listing (`MailReadController`) and the drafts listing
(`DraftController`, through `MailFacade.listDrafts`) — and the flat
list's last-page button asks for the last page, so one click asks for every
message between the mirror and the oldest one in the folder. The verification
pass measured the first step alone, against a server answering
`* 2000000 EXISTS`, which the B1-3 bound admits: `getMessages(1, 2000000)`
retained **394 MB**, 197 bytes a message, before a FETCH was sent. The FETCH
then holds a response per message (B1-8) and the parsed envelopes on top. An
honest folder takes the same path: its own size sets the range.

What the request downloads is not kept either. `MailboxMaintenanceService`
prunes a folder back to `mail.client.sync.local-window-limit` (10,000 rows)
after the next folder cycle, and the page-0 backfill already stops at that
limit for that reason; the lazy fetch alone ignores it.

**Severity: Medium**, B1-1's shape: a user action, routine in a large folder,
with the heap as its cost — on the packaged JVM an exit, which the client
restarts.

**Recommendation.** Never extend the mirror past the local window limit,
which is as deep as the pruner keeps it, and download the rest in batches of
the sync size. Decided with the owner at 1.16 (2026-09-27): the listing
paginates only that deep, its last page says the older mail is on the server
only, and the folder's message count keeps the server's number.

**Fix (shipped 2026-09-28).** The recommendation as decided.
`MailSyncService.lazyFetchPageRange` extends the mirror no deeper than
`listingDepth()` — the local window limit, 10,000 rows by default, as deep as
`MailboxMaintenanceService` keeps a folder — and takes the range in windows
of `mail.client.sync.window-size` (100), newest first, so one `getMessages`
and one FETCH hold a window rather than the range. With the mirror at the
depth it opens no folder at all. The listing says where it stops:
`MailFacade.getEmails`, which both listing endpoints go through, returns a
`WindowedPage`, whose total stays the server's count while its page count,
`hasNext` and `isLast` stop at the depth, and `PagedResponse` reports the
difference as `olderOnServer`. The flat list shows "Starší zprávy jsou jen na
serveru." on its last page and adds it to the page announcement. The page
number is still bounded only by `@Min(0)`; a page past the last one gets an
empty page and downloads nothing.

**Dynamic verification.** `MailSyncServiceTest` asks for the last page of a
1,790-message folder with a depth of 300 and 250 rows mirrored: one window,
sequence 1491–1540, and nothing else; with the mirror at the depth, no second
folder is opened. Against a lazy fetch without the depth both fail, which was
run and seen. A range down to an ordinary page goes in windows of 100, newest
first. `MailFacadeTest` lists page 15 of that folder at 20 a page: 1,790
messages, 15 pages, the last one, older mail on the server; without the
window it reports 90 pages, which was run and seen. `WindowedPageTest` pins
the arithmetic, and the e2e `list-navigation` spec pages to the end of a
folder the mock cuts at 20 of its 25 messages and finds the note on the page
and in the announcement.

**Residual.** Mail below the depth is on the server only, as the note says;
the listing did not keep it past the next prune before either.

**Status: fixed.**

## 4j. Finding B1-10 (Medium) — Angus parses a BODYSTRUCTURE by unbounded recursion — **FIXED**

**What.** The `BODYSTRUCTURE` constructor in Angus 2.0.5 builds the part tree
by calling itself once per level of nesting, with no limit. The structure is
the server's description of a message's MIME, so its depth is what the server
says, and the list sync asks for it in every batch (`CONTENT_INFO` in
`MessageFetcher`'s `FetchProfile`). Measured by the verification pass: parsed
in isolation, 5,000 levels (about 50 KB) overflow the stack and 3,000 do not;
over the wire, an unsolicited `* 1 FETCH (BODYSTRUCTURE …)` of 200,000 levels
(2,000,061 bytes, well under B1-7's 32 MiB) made `folder.open` throw
`StackOverflowError` through `BoundedImapStore`, and left the store connected.
Every handler of ours and of Angus's on the way catches exceptions, not errors:
`BoundedImapProtocol.readResponse` takes `RuntimeException`, Angus's
`Protocol.command` `IOException` and `ProtocolException`, `MessageFetcher`
`MessagingException`, and `ImapFolderExecutor`, the folder cycle and
`syncAllFolders` `Exception`. The error ends in Spring's `@Async`
interceptor around the pass, which takes any `Throwable` and only logs it
(no `AsyncConfigurer`, so `SimpleAsyncUncaughtExceptionHandler`).
One such message therefore ends the account's sync pass on every cycle,
before anything is recorded in `last_error` and before the folders after it
are synced. `-XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError` does not apply: the process stays
up, and the sync stops.

A hostile or compromised server can send it, and so can any sender whose
message reaches the recipient's server through a path that accepts MIME
nested that deeply and whose server then describes it so. Postfix, by
default, refuses mail nested deeper than 100 levels (`mime_nesting_limit`);
which other providers cap nesting, and where, was not checked.

**Severity: Medium** (_DoS_): automatic sync, no user interaction, and it
outlasts a restart, which the rubric's Medium line (_DoS recoverable by
restart_) does not describe. The rubric has no DoS line above it — High is
credential exposure or lasting loss of integrity, and nothing is lost here —
so it stays Medium on B1-3's reasoning, as B1-8 does.

**Recommendation.** Refuse the response before Angus parses it when its
parentheses nest deeper than a bound (the MIME walks' `MAX_DEPTH` is 20, far
below any stack limit), or treat an `Error` from the parse the way
`readResponse` treats a `RuntimeException` and close the connection. Either
way the sync has to be able to record the folder as failed and go on with the
next; whether the message itself can then be kept as an envelope-only stub,
as §2's malformed-structure path does, is a decision at fix time.

**Fix (shipped 2026-09-28).** The first of the two, and the stub. Catching
the `Error` was rejected: an overflow can land inside a class initializer and
leave that class unusable for the life of the process. `BoundedImapProtocol`
now performs `IMAPProtocol.readResponse`'s two steps itself, as Angus 2.0.5
does — the `IMAPResponse` constructor reads the whole response, then a FETCH
goes to the `FetchResponse` constructor, which parses its items, the
`BODYSTRUCTURE` among them — and between the two measures how deeply a FETCH's
parentheses nest. The count follows Angus's parser: a quoted string (escaped
quotes included) and a literal are data, a literal skipped by its declared
length, which the buffer already holds; it never recurses and stops at the
first level past the bound. Past `MAX_NESTING` = **256** levels the response
is dropped unparsed. A structure spends one level per MIME level and a few
around them, so 256 admits MIME nested some 250 deep, against the
extractor's 20 and Postfix's default 100, and stays an order of magnitude
under the 3,000 levels measured parsing on a test thread's stack. Only a FETCH
is measured, because only a FETCH is parsed further.

Dropped, not refused, and that was the decision the recommendation left open.
The sync downloads a folder's newest mail first, in windows, and advances its
cursor only past a window it stored, so a remedy that fails the command stops
the folder at the message on every cycle — and a sender's message is the
newest when it lands, so nothing that arrives after it syncs either — until
someone removes it on the server. A dropped response costs one message
instead. The drop is a `ProtocolException`, which `Protocol.command` logs and
skips (read from the 2.0.5 bytecode, as is `idleStart`'s identical handling;
where Angus waits for a continuation, a literal's or an authentication's, the
command fails as it would on any exception). Unlike the refusals of §4c and
§4g, which close the connection because a response they stop is read only in
part, this one has been read in full, so the connection is in step. Angus is
then left without that message's items: it loads the envelope, flags and
headers with FETCHes of their own, which do not nest, and loading the
structure fails with "Unable to load BODYSTRUCTURE", which the sync already
keeps as an envelope-only stub (§2). Opening it retries the body fetch, as
for any stub, and meets the same drop, so the content endpoint reports its own
failure.

Re-implementing Angus's two steps ties the class to 2.0.5's `readResponse`,
so `BoundedImapProtocolTest.angusIsTheVerifiedVersion` pins the Angus
version: a dependency update that moves it fails there until the hooks of
§4c, §4g and this section are re-read against the new bytecode.

**Dynamic verification.** `HostileImapResponseIT` sends the over-the-wire
case, an unsolicited FETCH whose `BODYSTRUCTURE` nests 200,000 levels (about
2 MB): the pass completes and records nothing. With the check disabled it
fails with the `StackOverflowError` escaping the pass, and with the response
refused rather than dropped it fails with the pass recorded as failed; both
were run and seen to fail. `MailSyncGreenMailIT` delivers a message nested 296
levels beside an ordinary one with an attachment: the pass succeeds, the
nested message is stored with its subject and sender and no attachments, and
the ordinary one with its attachment. It fails against the refusing variant,
which loses both messages; it passes with no check at all, because Angus
parses 296 levels without trouble — the overflow is the first test's
question. `BoundedImapProtocolTest` pins the count: the bound at its edge,
sibling lists not adding up, quoted strings and literals not counting, a
brace that starts no literal leaving what follows counted, a literal declared
past the end of the response ending the scan, and an ordinary multipart
structure with a parenthesised file name passing.

**Residual.** A message whose structure nests past 256 levels is listed by its
envelope and cannot be opened; its body and attachments stay on the server.
Since 1.32 so is one whose FETCH response names more than about 125 address
groups in all, across every envelope it carries (measured at 1.33, below).
A hostile server can also drop any message's items this way, which it can do
more simply by not sending them.

**Reopened at 1.24** (Medium, from the verification pass; read, not measured).
`nestsDeeperThan` skips a quoted string from a `"` to the next unescaped one,
as Angus's tokenizer does between items. But Angus reads the section of a
`BODY[section]` item with `readString(']')`, which takes a `"` inside the
brackets as an ordinary character. A FETCH carrying
`BODY[x"] NIL BODYSTRUCTURE ((((…` opens a quoted string for the check that
Angus never sees: the check skips the rest of the response, and the structure
it hid is parsed by recursion. **Recommendation:** skip what Angus skips — a
section inside `BODY[`…`]` up to its `]` — before counting depth.

**Fixed again at 1.27** (2026-09-29). Read from Angus 2.0.5's bytecode first:
`FetchResponse.parseItem` matches item names in any case, and takes `BODY`
followed by `[` as the `BODY` item, whose constructor reads the section with
`readString(']')` — every byte up to the next `]`, quotes, braces and
parentheses alike. No other item in this version reads a section. So
`nestsDeeperThan` now does the same: in the FETCH list itself, the one depth at
which Angus parses items, a `BODY[` in any case skips to the next `]`, or to
the end of the response where Angus fails the parse for want of one. That
covers a literal marker in a section too, which the check would have taken for
a literal and skipped the declared number of bytes past it. A `BODY[` at that
depth inside an item name Angus does not know is skipped as well, harmlessly:
Angus fails the parse at that name, before anything after it.

**Dynamic verification.** The bypass is now measured, not only read:
`HostileImapResponseIT` answers a folder open with a FETCH carrying
`BODY[x"] NIL` ahead of a structure nested 200,000 levels deep, and with the
section read as tokens the pass ends in a `StackOverflowError` from Angus's
`BODYSTRUCTURE` constructor, which was run and seen; with the fix the response
is dropped and the pass completes. `BoundedImapProtocolTest` holds a quote, a
lower-case item name and a literal marker in a section to the nesting after
it, and a section without its `]` to the end of the scan; with the section read
as tokens both tests fail, which was run and seen.

**Reopened at 1.31** (Medium, from the verification pass; measured). This
settles what the 1.27 fix left to verify — whether the check reads every
FETCH item the way Angus parses it — and the answer is no. Read from Angus
2.0.5's bytecode, `FetchResponse.parse` hands each item to a constructor of
its own, and several of them consume bytes without tokenizing them:

- `FLAGS` reads its list with `IMAPResponse.readSimpleList`, raw from `(` to
  the first `)`. A `"` or a `{n}` inside it is a flag's text to Angus and a
  string or a literal to the check; a `(` inside it leaves the check a level
  deeper than Angus, where it no longer looks for a `BODY[` section.
- A NIL is recognized by its first letter. `parseString`, which reads the
  strings, literals and NILs of `ENVELOPE`, `RFC822`, `BODY` and
  `BODYSTRUCTURE`, takes an `N` or `n` and skips the next two bytes unread, and
  `BODYSTRUCTURE`'s and `ENVELOPE`'s own NIL tests do the same.
- A literal is taken from its `{n}` without the CRLF the check requires:
  `parseString` skips the two bytes after `}` unread and then `n` more, where
  the check, finding no CRLF, reads on.
- `BODY` consumes the byte after its section's `]` whatever it is
  (`readByte`), and the byte after an origin's number (`skip(1)`).

Each lets a `"` stand where the check opens a string Angus never sees, so the
structure after it goes uncounted and Angus parses it by recursion. Measured
over TLS with a throwaway IT against `HostileImapServer`, each placed ahead of
the 200,000-level structure of the 1.17 test: `FLAGS (")`, `FLAGS ({n}` with
the literal holding `) BODYSTRUCTURE …`, `FLAGS (((a)` ahead of a `BODY[x"]`,
`RFC822 N"x`, `RFC822 {3}xx"ab`, `BODY[]"NIL` and `BODY[]<0" NIL`. All seven
end the pass in a `StackOverflowError` from the `BODYSTRUCTURE` constructor,
the 1.16 failure: the account's sync stops on every cycle with nothing
recorded. The same structure without them is dropped and the pass completes,
so the check was in place.

A sender cannot produce these. Each is malformed IMAP that a conforming
server does not write — a quote in a FLAGS list, a NIL that is not one, a
literal that does not end its line — so it takes a hostile or compromised
server, and the severity stays **Medium**, B1-10's own.

**Recommendation.** Stop modelling Angus's item parsers one reading at a
time: three versions of the check have now been bypassed by the next
reading. Either port into the scan what `FetchResponse.parse` and each item
constructor of 2.0.5 consume — the FLAGS list to its first `)`, a NIL as one
letter and two unread bytes, a literal from its `}` and two unread bytes,
BODY's byte after `]` and after an origin — pinned to the version as the
hooks already are; or count every parenthesis of the response, inside quoted
strings and literal data too, accepting that a message whose envelope,
structure or body chunk opens more than 256 then loses its structure or its
body. Skipping literal data alone is not safe: `FLAGS` reads on into a
literal's data. Which of the two is a decision at fix time.

**Fixed again at 1.32** (2026-10-05), by neither of the two: the count is no
longer taken from a scan before the parse but from Angus's parse itself.
Porting every item's reading, the first option, would have been a fourth
model of the same parser, open to the next reading it missed; counting every
parenthesis, the second, cannot hold either, since a `)` inside a string
lowers the count while Angus stays where it is, so a string of them before
each step down hides any depth. Read from Angus 2.0.5's source, every
recursion of the FETCH parse starts by consuming a `(` through a public,
overridable method of `Response`: a `BODYSTRUCTURE` with `readByte`, a
body-extension list with `skip(1)`, an address with `readByte`. So
`BoundedImapProtocol.DepthBoundedFetchResponse` is a `FetchResponse` whose
overrides of `readByte`, `skip` and `isNextNonSpace` count a `(` the parse
consumes as opening a level and a `)` it consumes that way as closing one,
and stop the parse once more than `MAX_NESTING` (256) levels are open. What
the parse reads as data — a quoted string, a literal, a `BODY` section, a
FLAGS list, the bytes after a NIL's first letter — it reads by moving its
index, past these methods, so no byte of data counts, whatever it holds. Two
consumptions needed care. `readStringList` opens its list by moving the index
and closes it with `isNextNonSpace(')')`, so the overrides do not count inside
it; and `BODY` consumes the byte after its section with `readByte`, which
can be a `)` Angus takes as data, but only at the top of the FETCH list, and
the count stops at zero, so it can hide one level at most.

The same reading found a second recursion no count of parentheses sees, the
first finding of 1.32 under B1-10's number. An `ENVELOPE`'s address list is
parsed by `IMAPAddress`, and an address that starts a group — a NIL host
and a mailbox naming the group — parses the group's members inside its own
constructor, so a member that starts a group recurses again. Each address
closes its parentheses before the next begins, so the nesting stays at one
level however deep the groups go. Measured on a test thread's stack: 3,000
such groups parse, and 10,000, 170 KB, overflow it, under the response bound
by two orders of magnitude. The earlier scan did not flag them at any depth
tried, up to 300,000, and the `ENVELOPE` is in every list sync's FETCH. A
conforming server writes this only from headers that nest groups, which
RFC 5322 does not allow; whether any server does so from a sender's headers
was not checked, so the severity is B1-10's own, **Medium**. The bound counts
these too: an address is a `(`, four `readString`s and a `)`, and starts or
ends a group exactly when the fourth, the host, is NIL; each such address
adds a level that is never taken back, since nothing Angus consumes says where
a group ends. 256 then admits an envelope naming a hundred groups.

A miscount can go one way only: a level counted that Angus did not open
costs an honest response at worst, and every level Angus opens is counted
by the rule above for this version, which `angusIsTheVerifiedVersion` pins.

**Dynamic verification.** `BoundedImapProtocolTest` parses through the same
path: the bound at its edge, 1,024 sibling parts, parentheses in a quoted
string, a literal and a FLAGS list not counting, every shape of 1.24 and 1.31
ahead of a structure 20,000 levels deep, address groups nested 256 and 20,000
deep, and ordinary groups and structures with extension data. With the parse
unbounded, the structure, the shapes and the groups end in
`StackOverflowError` and the edge is not dropped, which was run and seen.
`HostileImapResponseIT` adds two cases over the wire, `FLAGS (")` ahead of the
200,000-level structure and 100,000 nested groups: the pass completes and
records nothing, and with the parse unbounded both end in
`StackOverflowError`, run and seen.

**Verified at 1.33** (the independent pass, against `55f4afa`; read from
Angus 2.0.5's sources jar, then measured). The three claims the fix rests on
hold for this version:

- _Every recursion starts by consuming a `(` through a counted method._ The
  FETCH parse recurses in three places, all read: `BODYSTRUCTURE`'s
  constructor (`readByte`, for a multipart's parts and a `message/rfc822`'s
  body), `parseBodyExtension` (`skip(1)` over a peeked `(`) and `IMAPAddress`
  inside a group, counted by its NIL host. `FetchResponse` is built only in
  `IMAPProtocol.readResponse`, which this class replaces, and
  `IMAPProtocol.getFetchItems()` is empty, so no extension item parses
  anything; `MODSEQ` opens and closes its list through `readByte` and
  `isNextNonSpace`, and `FLAGS`, `INTERNALDATE`, `RFC822*`, `UID` and the
  `BODY` data read by moving the index.
- _A counted `)` ends a list the same frame opened, but for one._ Every
  `readByte` that can return `)` either throws on it (a structure's, an
  envelope's, an address list's or an address's opening, a parameter list, a
  single part's disposition) or ends the structure frame it is read in (the
  `while (r.readByte() == ' ')` loops, whose first other byte Angus takes as
  the frame's end); every `isNextNonSpace(')')` closes the list or frame that
  opened with a counted `(`, except inside `readStringList`, which is not
  counted at either end. The exception is `BODY`'s byte after `]`, at the top
  of the FETCH list only. Where Angus leaves a frame on a byte that is not `)`,
  or on a `(`, the count stays higher than the depth — the safe direction.
- _Data does not pass through the counted methods to hide depth._ A literal, a
  quoted string, a NIL's last two bytes and a `BODY` section move the index
  directly; `skip` counts a `(` it passes over and never a `)`.

Measured with a throwaway test through `BoundedImapProtocol.parseFetch`, each
shape ahead of or around a structure 20,000 levels deep, with Angus's plain
`FetchResponse` beside it: a thousand `BODY[])` items, alternating
`BODY[]<0(` and `BODY[])`, a language list broken by a NIL at the leaf and at
every level, extension lists nested 20,000 deep in a single part and in the
Exchange workaround's branch, a chain of 20,000 `message/rfc822` parts, and
address groups. Every one was dropped by the bounded parse and overflowed the
stack in the plain one. No shape kept the count at or under 256 while Angus
went deeper than the one level the top-level `BODY` allows.

Honest structures can be dropped one way the section does not say: a group
marker is never taken back, and every envelope in the response adds its own,
so they add up across sibling parts rather than along one path. A multipart
carrying forwarded messages (`message/rfc822`), each with an envelope whose To
is `undisclosed-recipients:;` — two markers — parsed with 100 of them and was
dropped with 126, measured; plain Angus parses both, since the groups do not
nest. Such a message lists by its envelope and cannot be opened, the residual above. Rare enough for a mail client that the bound stands, but the residual covers it too.

**Fixed at 1.35** (#631), the honest half above. Groups nest only inside
one address list: the recursion runs in the list's own loop, where the count
of lists never drops below the list's level, and has returned by the time the
list's `)` is consumed. So `DepthBoundedFetchResponse` remembers the lowest
level at which a group marker now counted was read, and forgets the markers
once the count of lists drops below it; an envelope's groups no longer add up
with the next envelope's, while groups nested within one list still count as
before. `BoundedImapProtocolTest` parses a multipart forwarding 200 such
messages; with the markers kept it is dropped, which was run and seen, and the nested-groups tests still drop at 256 and at 20,000. **Confirmed by the pass over 1.37** (#635), from Angus 2.0.5's source: a group's members are parsed inside the group start's own loop, entirely within the address list that opened with a counted `(`, and the `)` that closes that list — the only close that can take the count below the level the markers were read at — is consumed after every address frame of the list has returned, so no group frame is live when the markers are forgotten.

The same reading found an unbounded loop in the same parse, not a recursion,
which no depth count can see: **B1-15** (§4o).

**Status: fixed at 1.32, confirmed at 1.33.** Fixed at 1.17 and 1.27;
reopened at 1.24, for a quote in a `BODY` section, and at 1.31, for the other
items.

## 4k. Finding B1-11 (Low) — a sender label with `>` before `<` breaks opening the message — **FIXED**

**What.** `MessageEntity.getFromEmailOnly` recovers the address from the stored
`Personal <email>` label as the text between the first `<` and the first `>`.
When the personal part holds a `>` — `From: "Shop > News" <x@y>` — the first
`>` comes before the first `<`, and `substring` throws
`StringIndexOutOfBoundsException`. The content endpoint reads the address on
every open (`MailFacade`), so the message cannot be opened, and a reply to it
cannot be prefilled (`MailDraftService`). Any sender can choose such a name.

**Severity: Low**: one message, nothing lost, and the sender gains a message
that will not open. It rests on the same recovery as the §5 note on the `From`
label, and storing the parsed address instead of recovering it would close
both.

**Fix (shipped 2026-09-28).** Not by storing the address: the label already
carries it unambiguously, because `MessageFetcher.formatAddress` writes it
last, as `personal <address>`, so whatever brackets the personal part holds
come before the label's last `<`. `HeaderAddresses.labelAddress` reads the
address from there — the text between the last `<` and the closing `>` of a
label that ends with one, else the label itself — and
`MessageEntity.getFromEmailOnly` calls it, which covers the content endpoint,
the reply prefill and the allow-list key. A `>` inside a quoted local part
stays in the address. The one address it still misreads is one whose quoted
local part holds a `<` (`"a<b"@x`), which a sender can only aim at their own
reply target; nothing throws on any label. The fourth use read the label
another way: `CorrespondentService` tokenized it as a header field for the
typeahead, which split a name holding a comma (`Novak, Jan`) and harvested a
name's own address (`Alice <a@evil> <a@real>` put `a@evil` into the
typeahead under the name Alice). It now reads the sender through
`HeaderAddresses.parseLabel`, the same rule with the text before the address
as the name, and the typeahead keeps only an address that validates. That
closes the §5 note on the `From` label as well. No schema change: storing the
address would have added a column for what the label already says.

**Dynamic verification.** `MailDraftServiceTest` prefills a reply from labels
that `formatAddress` builds for `"Shop > News" <news@shop.example>` and for
`"Alice <alice@evil.example>" <alice@example.com>`: against the old recovery
the first throws `StringIndexOutOfBoundsException` and the second replies to
`alice@evil.example`; both were run that way and seen to fail.
`CorrespondentServiceTest` harvests the second label and `Novak, Jan <…>`;
against the old tokenizing both fail, which was run and seen.
`HeaderAddressesTest` pins the edges: a `>` in a quoted local part, a label
without brackets, an unclosed one, an empty address.

**Residual.** A sender address whose quoted local part holds a `<` is read
wrongly, to the sender's own cost.

**Reopened at 1.24** (Low, from the verification pass; read). A fifth reader of
the label was missed, outside `Code paths`: the frontend's add-contact
prefill, `frontend/src/lib/contacts/senderContact.ts`, still takes the text
between the label's first `<` and first `>`, so a name holding an address of
its own prefills the contact form with it. The user sees the form, and can
correct it, before saving. **Recommendation:** read the last `<…>`, as
`HeaderAddresses.labelAddress` does.

**Fixed again at 1.28** (2026-10-04), as recommended: `senderContactSeed`
takes the address from the label's last `<` to a closing `>` that ends the
label, and the label itself otherwise, with the text before that `<` as the
name — `HeaderAddresses.labelAddress` and `parseLabel`'s rule. The file joins
`Code paths`, so the fifth reader is under the freshness check with the
other four. **Dynamic verification:** `senderContact.test.ts` seeds a contact
from `Alice <alice@evil.example> <alice@example.com>`, from
`Shop > News <news@shop.example>` and from a label with text after its
brackets; against the old reading the first prefilled `alice@evil.example`,
the second seeded nothing, which hides the add-contact offer, and the third accepted the label, which was
run and seen.

**Confirmed at 1.31**, by reading. `senderContactSeed` takes the label apart
as `HeaderAddresses.labelAddress` and `parseLabel` do: the last `<` when the
trimmed label ends with `>`, the label itself otherwise, and the text before
that `<` as the name. No sixth reader was found: the frontend's other uses of
the sender label display it. `senderContact.test.ts` and `HeaderAddressesTest`
pass at `64633fe`.

**Status: fixed at 1.28**, with the residual above.

## 4l. Finding B1-12 (Medium) — the attachment lookup reads a text part on the path whole into memory — **FIXED**

**What.** `AttachmentService.findPartByPath` asks each part it descends
through for its `getContent()` before looking at what it is — the message
itself, then every part on the path but the one it returns. For a multipart
that is the `Multipart`, and for most single parts a stream. For a `text/plain`, `text/html` or `text/xml`
part it is the whole body decoded into a `String`, sized by the server. So a
message whose only body is text is read into the heap whatever path is asked
for, before `Files.copy` streams the part a second time from
`getInputStream()`, and so is a text part the path runs through, and the
download §5 calls disk-bounded is not. #582 made the body of a single-part
message downloadable as part 1 — the path the extractor gives an attachment
that is a whole message's body — but did not add the read: the lookup made
the same call before #582 and then failed.

**Severity: Medium**, B1-1's shape before its fix: a user action, a body of
the server's choosing read whole into the heap, which nothing bounds — B1-1's
8 MiB cap is the extractor's and B1-7's 32 MiB is one response — and on the
packaged JVM an exit, which the client restarts. B1-9 is Medium on the same
grounds.

**Recommendation.** Decide from the part's type, and ask for content only
where it is a multipart or an encapsulated message.

**Fix (shipped 2026-09-28).** As recommended: `findPartByPath` asks a part
for its content only when `isMimeType` says it is a `multipart/*` or a
`message/rfc822`, the two types that hold parts. A text body is therefore
streamed once, by `Files.copy`, whatever its size, and a path that runs
through a text part ends as a missing part without reading it. For a message
from the server the content of those two types is Angus's structure — an
`IMAPMultipartDataSource` or an `IMAPNestedMessage` built from the
`BODYSTRUCTURE` — so the lookup reads no body at all. A multipart whose
content is not a `Multipart` falls through as before, so a body that is the
whole message is still part 1.

**Dynamic verification.** `AttachmentServiceTest` downloads a single-part
text message as part 1, asks for part 1.1 of it, and checks that
`getContent()` is never called on the message. Against the unfixed lookup it
fails there, with the call made twice; it was run that way and seen to fail.

**Residual.** None.

**Reopened at 1.24** (Medium, from the verification pass; read from Angus
2.0.5's bytecode, not measured). `isMimeType` decides by the type the
BODYSTRUCTURE names, but what `getContent()` returns depends on how Angus
processed the structure. One that names `multipart/*` in the single-part form,
or `message/rfc822` without an envelope, leaves `IMAPMessage.getDataHandler`
to fall through to `MimeMessage`'s own handler, and `getContent()` then reads
the whole part and parses it in memory — through `MimeMultipart`'s preamble,
or `MimeMessage(Session, InputStream)`. The "reads no body at all" and
"Residual: None" above do not hold against a server that describes a part so.
**Recommendation:** ask for content only where Angus built a structure-backed
object, judged by the structure it parsed rather than by the declared type,
and otherwise treat the part as a leaf.

**Still open at 1.31**, re-read rather than measured. `findPartByPath` still
decides by `isMimeType` (#608 changed only the message keys its exceptions
name), and in Angus 2.0.5's bytecode `IMAPMessage.getDataHandler` builds an
`IMAPMultipartDataSource` only for a structure parsed in multipart form
(`BODYSTRUCTURE.isMulti`) and an `IMAPNestedMessage` only for a nested one
with an envelope on an IMAP4rev1 server; everything else falls through to
`MimeMessage`'s own handler.

**Fixed again at 1.34** (2026-10-05), as recommended. `findPartByPath` asks
a part for its content through `holdsParts`: the declared type still has to
be `multipart/*` or `message/rfc822`, and for a part on the server — an
`IMAPMessage`, which `IMAPNestedMessage` is, or an `IMAPBodyPart` — the
handler must not be `MimeMessage`'s own. Angus 2.0.5's `getDataHandler`
builds an `IMAPMultipartDataSource` or an object handler holding an
`IMAPNestedMessage` from the parsed structure, and otherwise returns the
superclass handler over a `MimePartDataSource`, whose content is the part
fetched whole and parsed. That one is now a leaf: a message is still its own
part 1, streamed by `Files.copy` from `getInputStream`, and a path below it
is not found. Asking for the handler reads no body: it loads the
`BODYSTRUCTURE` at most, a FETCH bounded like any other (§4j). A part not on
the server — a message parsed from bytes already in memory, as the tests'
are — is judged by its type alone, as before.

**Dynamic verification.** `AttachmentServiceTest` gives the download an
`IMAPMessage` declared `multipart/mixed` whose handler is the
`MimePartDataSource` one: part 1 streams its body, part 1.1 is not found, and
`getContent()` is never called. Against the 1.18 lookup it fails there, with
the call made, which was run and seen. A second test gives one whose handler holds the parts as an object, as Angus's are, and finds its part 2 — wrongly: Angus's multipart handler is not an object handler (see below). Not done:
the over-the-wire shape against `HostileImapServer`, which serves the sync
and not the attachment download; the handler choice is read from Angus's
source, as the reopening was.

**Residual.** A message the server describes so is listed and opens as
before; the attachments inside its body cannot be downloaded one by one.

**Regression at 1.34, fixed at 1.38** (2026-10-05, #634). Found by the
independent pass begun over 1.37, read from Angus 2.0.5's source:
`IMAPMultipartDataSource` extends `MimePartDataSource`, to implement
`MultipartDataSource`. `holdsParts` tested for that superclass alone, so
it made every multipart Angus built from the structure a leaf, the case the
fix was meant to keep: a message with an attachment downloaded its body as
part 1, and every attachment in it was "not found". It never shipped — the
`v0.1.0` tag predates #630 — but `main` carried it from #630 to this
change. The test that should have caught it modelled Angus's handler as an
object holding the parts, which is the nested message's shape, not the
multipart's. `holdsParts` now recognizes a `MultipartDataSource` first and
treats only a `MimePartDataSource` that is not one as the fall-through.

**Dynamic verification (1.38).** `AttachmentServiceTest` gives the download
an `IMAPMessage` whose handler is Angus's own `IMAPMultipartDataSource`
(an anonymous subclass, its constructor being protected) and finds its part
2; against the 1.34 lookup it fails with "MIME part 2 was not found", which
was run and seen. A nested message held by an object handler is descended
into as well, and the fall-through test still passes.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.39** (a separate session's), against Angus
2.0.5's and jakarta.mail 2.1.5's bytecode. `IMAPMultipartDataSource` extends
`MimePartDataSource` and implements `MultipartDataSource`.
`IMAPMessage.getDataHandler` builds it only for a structure parsed in
multipart form (`BODYSTRUCTURE.isMulti`), an object handler over an
`IMAPNestedMessage` only for a nested structure with an envelope on an
IMAP4rev1 server, and otherwise — or once the body is loaded — returns
`MimeMessage`'s own handler; `IMAPBodyPart.getDataHandler` makes the same
three choices. Angus's `multipart_mixed` handler builds a `MimeMultipart`
over the data source, which for a `MultipartDataSource` takes its parts from
it and parses nothing, so descending into a multipart Angus built reads no
body. The body counts as loaded only after a FETCH carried it whole
(`handleFetchItem`), which no request of this app asks for; a server sending
one unasked has already spent that memory (§4n), and the message is then a
leaf. Both directions were run and seen: with the 1.34 test,
`aMultipartFromTheStructureIsDescended` fails with "MIME part 2 was not
found"; with the type alone deciding, `aMultipartWithoutAStructureIsALeaf`
fails with `getContent()` called.

**Status: fixed at 1.38**, confirmed by the pass over 1.39. Reopened at
1.24; regressed at 1.34.

## 4m. Finding B1-13 (Medium) — each EXPUNGE costs a pass over the folder, and a server can send them without end — **FIXED**

**What.** Angus's `MessageCache.expungeMessage` shifts the sequence-number
array on every EXPUNGE, and `msgnumOf` finds a number by linear scan, so one
EXPUNGE costs time in proportion to the folder's size. A server can send
`* 1 EXPUNGE` over and over: at 2,000,000 messages, k of them cost about
k × 2,000,000 steps, and a later EXISTS refills the folder for another round.
The budget admits about 229,000 per command; until 1.25, the streamed listing
(`readEach`) did not charge them at all. The read timeout does not stop it: the
server keeps answering. Found by the 1.24 verification pass, read from Angus
2.0.5's bytecode; not measured.

**Severity: Medium** (DoS), on B1-3's reasoning: automatic sync, a hostile
server, the sync thread and the account's lane busy for as long as the server
keeps it up, and a restart meets the same folder.

**Recommendation.** Charge an EXPUNGE for the work it causes — the folder's
size, which the protocol sees in EXISTS — or bound how many EXPUNGE responses
one command may bring, far above what this client's own deletes produce.

**Measured at 1.26**, over TLS through `BoundedImapStore` on the desktop
machine (Ryzen 9 9900X): with a folder open at 2,000,000 messages, EXPUNGEs
riding on the UID listing took 240 ms for 500, 453 ms for 1,000, 891 ms for
2,000 and 1,779 ms for 4,000 — 0.45 ms each, linear in their number. At the
229,000 the command budget admitted, that is some 100 seconds a command, and
the sync sends a folder dozens of commands. The same EXPUNGEs in a SELECT's own
reply cost nothing — 2,000 in 8 ms — because Angus registers the folder's
handler only once the SELECT completes; the cost falls on every later command.

**Fix (1.26, 2026-09-29).** The first half of the recommendation, over the
selected folder rather than one command, since the commands a sync sends to one
open folder add up. `BoundedImapProtocol` keeps a `SelectionBudget`, started
by the SELECT or EXAMINE that selects a folder (`writeCommand`). It charges each
EXPUNGE, and each UID of a live VANISHED — the form Angus expunges messages for
— the length Angus's array may have reached: the largest EXISTS since the
selection, plus the expunged entries Angus keeps in it until the folder closes.
Past `MAX_SELECTION_EXPUNGE_STEPS` = 1,000 × `MAX_MESSAGES` = 2,000,000,000
steps the response is refused as B1-3's are: the connection closed, the command
failed. That is 999 EXPUNGEs at 2,000,000 messages, about half a second at the
measured rate, or some 46,000 in a folder of 20,000 (1.26–1.30 said a hundred
thousand, which leaves out the expunged entries each EXPUNGE is also charged
for; the javadoc of `MAX_SELECTION_EXPUNGE_STEPS` still says so). The charge is an
upper bound — Angus ignores an EXPUNGE past its count and a VANISHED UID it does
not know — and the EARLIER form is not charged, since Angus expands it without
expunging. The value is the author's, under the owner's standing decision of
2026-09-28 to take such values from the bounds already in the class: this
client expunges one message per folder it opens (`ImapActionService`), and
more arrive only from other clients while the folder is selected.

**Dynamic verification.** `HostileImapResponseIT` opens a folder at 2,000,000
messages and sends 2,000 `* 1 EXPUNGE` ahead of the UID listing: the command
fails with the selection budget's refusal; with the charge switched off it
completes, which was run and seen. It sends 600 on each of two opens of the
same folder, and both complete, because each SELECT starts a new budget; with
the budget kept across selections the second open is refused, which was run
and seen. `BoundedImapProtocolTest` pins the arithmetic: 999 EXPUNGEs at
`MAX_MESSAGES`, the largest EXISTS rather than the latest, a live VANISHED
charged per UID and the EARLIER form not at all, and every other response free.

**Residual.** A hostile server still gets its half second per folder open — on
the notebook machine, which measures about half as fast, a second — once per
cycle, as it gets whatever a slow answer costs. An honest burst past the budget,
another client expunging a thousand messages from a folder of two million while
this one has it selected, costs a retried cycle, not the folder: the
reconnected SELECT reports what is left in its EXISTS count, with no EXPUNGE to
process.

**Confirmed at 1.31**, by reading and by the suites above, which pass at
`64633fe`. A selection budget starts only at a SELECT or EXAMINE the client
writes, and is charged for every response the connection reads, the FETCH
responses `readEach` leaves uncharged included: the selection charge comes
before the command budget's exemption. The bounds were recounted with the
class's own charge, one EXPUNGE at a time, which gave the 999 above and the
corrected 46,000.

**Status: fixed at 1.26**, with the residual above.

## 4n. Finding B1-14 (Medium) — what the folder keeps from FETCH responses adds up across the commands of one open folder — **FIXED**

**What.** `IMAPFolder`'s response handler records what each FETCH it is given
names: an `IMAPMessage` for the sequence number, up to the folder's EXISTS
count, and a UID-table entry for every UID it has not yet seen on that message
— the entry for the old UID stays. Both last until the folder closes (read from
Angus 2.0.5's bytecode). B1-8's budget bounds one command's responses while the
command runs; what the handler kept from them outlives it. Measured at 1.25:
`HostileImapResponseIT` answers one `getMessageByUID` with 100,000 lines
renaming the folder's one message — about 30 MB of the 64 MiB budget, so
admitted — and the folder's UID table holds 100,000 entries after the command
ends. A sync pass opens a folder once and, for new mail, sends two commands
per window of 100 UIDs — the `getMessagesByUID` that names the window and the
`fetch` that loads it — up to the 10,000-message local window: at least two
hundred commands, each of which can leave some 220,000 entries, over forty
million in all, at about 50 bytes each by object layout (not measured) — past
the heap. (1.25–1.30 said a command per batch of 200 and some fifty commands,
eleven million entries; `downloadRangeInternal` goes by `window-size`, not
`batch-size`. The conclusion is the same.) With EXISTS at the
2,000,000 B1-3 admits, FETCH responses naming every sequence number make an
`IMAPMessage` for each as well. Found by the author while fixing B1-8's
reopening; the streamed listing, its largest instance, no longer hands its
responses to the handler (§4h).

**Widened at 1.27**, by the author's further reading of Angus 2.0.5's bytecode
and of the sync; neither half is measured. What the folder keeps is data, not
only entries: `IMAPFolder.fetch` applies each FETCH response in its answer to
the message whose sequence number the response names, whether or not that
message was asked for, so one `fetch` a hostile server answers can attach up
to the command budget's 64 MiB of envelopes and structures to messages the
folder holds until it closes, and the next command as much again. And an honest
server reaches the same place: the incremental sync downloads every message
that arrived since the last cycle in one open folder
(`MessageDownloader.downloadRangeInternal`, windows of 100 UIDs with no count
bound), and the folder keeps each downloaded message's envelope and structure
until it closes. A catch-up after another client moved a few hundred thousand
messages into a folder holds all of them at once, and the out-of-memory exit
restarts it from the same UID, since the last known UID moves only once the
download completes. The pruner then keeps the newest `local-window-limit`
anyway (`MailboxMaintenanceService`), so everything below that was downloaded
only to be deleted.

**Severity: Medium** (DoS), on B1-8's reasoning: automatic sync, a hostile
server, the out-of-memory exit, and a restart meets the same folder.

**Recommendation.** Two halves, as for B1-8. Stop the honest sync from
reaching the bound: download no more new messages in one open folder than the
pruner keeps, newest first, which leaves the local mirror as it is today after
the prune. Then bound, per open folder, what the charged FETCH responses may
leave behind — counted from the SELECT or EXAMINE that opened it, in bytes and
not only in responses — at a size the client's own commands fit under: the
sync's batches, the lazy page fetch, and a message opened with its
attachments. The size is a decision at fix time, after measuring what an
honest pass of that shape carries.

**Honest half fixed at 1.30** (2026-10-04), as recommended:
`MessageDownloader.downloadRangeInternal` downloads at most
`local-window-limit` new messages in one open folder, newest first, and the
older new messages stay on the server, below the window, where the lazy page
fetch reaches them. When the limit cuts a catch-up short, every row of the
folder below the oldest message the catch-up reached is deleted at once, by
UID: the same pass looks for holes in the mirrored UID window before the
pruner runs, and would take the gap of skipped messages for one and download
it after all. By UID rather than by count, so a message the fetch dropped
cannot leave an old row standing below the gap. The mirror ends as the pruner
leaves it.

**Correction to 1.27.** An out-of-memory exit does not restart the catch-up
from the same UID. Each batch advances the last known UID, and the first
batch is the newest, so the cursor reaches the top at once; the messages a
crash leaves behind become a hole, which `reconcileServerOnlyUids` downloads,
with no count bound, the next time the deletion cleanup runs. The cap makes
such a crash far less likely; the reconcile path itself is still unbounded.

**Dynamic verification (1.30).** `MessageDownloaderTest` runs a catch-up of
four new messages against a window of three: three come down, newest first,
the fourth is never fetched, and the rows below the oldest of them are
deleted. Against the old download all four came down, which was run and
seen. A catch-up within the window comes down whole and deletes nothing.

**Verified at 1.31.** The honest half, re-derived from
`downloadRangeInternal`: windows from the newest UID down, the count checked
before each window, the last window trimmed to its newest messages, and —
only when the range was cut short — `deleteOlderThan` by the UID of the
oldest message reached, before `syncFlagsAndDeletions` looks for holes; the
pruner runs after the cycle (`enforceLocalWindowLimitAsync`). The count is of
messages the fetch mapped, not of messages the folder was handed, so a window
whose fetch maps nothing does not count towards it; that is the hostile
half's question, not the honest one's. The 1.30 correction holds:
`saveMessagesBatchAtomic` moves the last known UID to the top of each batch
and the first batch is the newest, and `reconcileServerOnlyUids` downloads
every hole the UID listing reveals, in batches of `batch-size` within one
open folder, with no count bound but the listing's own `MAX_MESSAGES`. The
hostile half was measured again with the throwaway IT: one `getMessageByUID`
answered with 100,000 lines renaming the folder's one message left 100,000
entries in its UID table, as at 1.25. `MessageDownloaderTest` passes at
`64633fe`.

**The reconcile path, bounded at 1.37** (2026-10-05, #633).
`reconcileServerOnlyUids` now takes how many messages the pass has already
downloaded, and the holes share the local window's room with them. With no
room left they wait for the next pass, whose catch-up is then empty; with
more holes than room only the newest come down — the holes arrive sorted by
UID — and the folder's rows below the oldest of them are deleted by UID,
`deleteOlderThan`, as a catch-up cut short leaves the mirror. The older holes
and the rows among them stay on the server, where the lazy page fetch reaches
them. So the honest paths of one pass together bring at most
`local-window-limit` messages into the open folder: the gap a crash
mid-catch-up leaves, or one another client opens inside the window, comes
down a window at a time at most.

**Dynamic verification (1.37).** `MessageDownloaderTest` hands the reconcile
four holes with room for two: only the newest two are fetched, and the rows
below the older of them are deleted. Holes that fit come down whole and
delete nothing; a pass with no room left fetches and deletes nothing. With the room unbounded the first and the last fail, which was run and seen.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.37** (#635): the hole list is sorted ascending on the one path that builds it (`detectServerOnlyHolesInWindow`, the others are empty), the room is the window less the pass's own count, which `MailSyncService` passes, and `deleteOlderThan` is scoped to the account and folder and to UIDs below the threshold, so it cannot reach another folder or the rows above the window it keeps.

**Fix of the hostile half (1.49, 2026-10-06, #650).** As recommended, per
open folder and in bytes. B1-13's `SelectionBudget`, started by the SELECT or
EXAMINE that selects the folder, also sums what the folder keeps of the FETCH
responses handed to it, and the response that passes the budget is refused as
B1-3's are: the connection closed, which releases everything the folder kept.
`BoundedImapProtocol.keptBytes` estimates what a response leaves as heap: its
bytes on the wire, 96 bytes (`PARSED_OBJECT_BYTES`) for each object Angus's
parse made, and B1-8's 280 for the response. The objects are counted in
`DepthBoundedFetchResponse`, from the same methods B1-10 counts levels in:
every string, byte array and list read, and every list opened, a `FLAGS` item
twice, for the lower-cased copy Angus keeps of a user flag. Per byte on the
wire the kept items cost 2.5 to 18 times their size (measured, below), so a
charge by bytes alone would have been one a server could exceed eighteen-fold.

Two kinds of response go uncharged. The FETCH responses `readEach` streams to
its caller reach no handler, as for B1-8. And the body data of a fetch of
content: every body section Angus fetches passes `fetchSectionBody`, or
`fetchRFC822` on an IMAP4 server, and the folder's handler takes only the UID,
the MODSEQ and the flags from a response (`IMAPFolder.processFetchResponse`,
read from 2.0.5's bytecode), so the content of a message or a part, read in
16 KiB pieces, goes to a caller that lets it go. Headers fetched that way are
charged, since `IMAPMessage` and `IMAPBodyPart` keep what they load
(`HEADER`, `HEADER.FIELDS (…)`, a part's `MIME`). Which of the two a fetch is
goes by the section the client asked for, not the one the answer names:
`fetchSectionBody` returns the one `BODY` item an answer carries for the
message whatever section it names (read), so a server could otherwise label
headers as content and have them kept uncharged. The fix's first draft went by
the answer's label, which on an honest server also left a part's `MIME`
headers uncharged, since Angus counts as headers only a section that starts
with `HEADER`.

**The value.** 64 MiB (`DEFAULT_OPEN_FOLDER_BUDGET`), configurable as
`mail.client.imap.open-folder-budget` in `application.properties` or, without
a new build, as the environment variable `MAIL_CLIENT_IMAP_OPENFOLDERBUDGET`,
which the packaged sidecar inherits: the shell plugin adds the variables the
frontend passes to the inherited ones (`tauri-plugin-shell` 2.4.0, read). The
approach is the owner's (2026-10-06): a value set by estimate, configurable,
refined from what the beta measures, as B1-8's values were set on 2026-09-28.
The largest honest selection is a sync pass that catches up
`local-window-limit` (10,000) new messages: 88 MB at the 8.8 KB an ordinary
message is charged (an envelope with five addresses, a three-part structure,
three threading headers; 6.7 KB kept, measured), and on a server without
CONDSTORE some 1.5 KB more for each mirrored message, whose flags and UIDs the
pass reads through the folder — up to twice the window before the pruner runs,
30 MB (computed with `keptBytes`, not measured). The author first set the
budget at 128 MiB, which that pass fits; the owner lowered it to 64 MiB the
same day, for the residual below, so that pass is cut short on purpose and
goes on at the next attempt (measured below at a smaller scale), and ordinary
new mail fits many times over. The floor: the budget has to hold what a pass
spends on the window it already mirrors, plus one batch — up to some 30 MB and
2 MB at the defaults — and below that every attempt is refused at the same
point and the folder's sync stops there (measured below). A configured zero
fails the startup. A refusal logs the budget and the key, and a folder open
that passes half of it logs so once, so a tester's log shows a folder nearing
it before it is refused.

**Measured at 1.49**, Angus 2.0.5, retained heap after a forced collection.
Per object, the heap of the items a folder keeps less their bytes on the wire:
82 bytes for a bare UID, 73 for an ordinary message, 66 for a structure dense
with parameters, 67 for a capitalised flag counted twice, 25 for an envelope
dense with addresses — each under the 96 charged. A response that makes Angus
create a message, each naming a new sequence number under a large EXISTS,
over TLS through `BoundedImapStore`: 40,000 of them kept 263 and 268 bytes
each in two runs, the message and its UID-table entry, against the 403 each is
charged. So what a hostile server can make one open folder keep is at most
some 0.86 of the budget, about 58 MB at the default.

**Dynamic verification (1.49).** `HostileImapResponseIT` configures the budget
at 16 MB, so its refusals show the configured value is the one in force.
Commands renaming the folder's one message 30,000 times each: the first is
admitted and logs the half-budget warning, the second is refused by the
budget; with the refusal switched off all three complete, run and seen. Each
open of the folder starts its own budget. A hundred 256 KiB pieces of a part's
content, 25 MB, come down in one open; a hundred fetches of the part's `MIME`
headers, each answered as `BODY[1]`, are refused, and with the charge going by
the answer's label, as in the first draft, they complete, run and seen.
`BoundedImapProtocolTest` pins the charge and which sections are content,
`ImapPropertiesTest` binds the key and the environment variable and refuses a
zero, and the connection manager's and the probe's tests check that the
budget is in the session before its store is asked for.
`OpenFolderBudgetGreenMailIT` catches up 400 new messages over GreenMail,
which advertises no CONDSTORE, at a 1 MB budget with windows and batches of
50: the budget refused two attempts, the batches each had stored stayed, and
the third attempt brought the rest down as holes — 405 messages mirrored and
the pass clean. At 96 KB, under one batch, five passes stayed at the five
messages mirrored before, run and seen.

**Residual.** The budget is per connection, and an account has two that may
each hold a folder open, the background lane's and the interactive lane's,
while a command in progress may hold up to its own 64 MiB of responses besides
(B1-8). A server that drives both lanes to their bounds at once — which needs
the user to act on the account while its sync is held — could make the
account hold some 2 × (58 + 67) MB, about two thirds of the packaged 384 MB
heap; at the 128 MiB first set it was 2 × (115 + 67), most of it, which is why
the owner lowered the default. A budget per account across its connections
would close it; not done here (owner's decision, 2026-10-06). And the charge
is an estimate, an upper bound for every shape measured; a shape that keeps
more than it is charged would let a server past the budget by that ratio.

**The pass over 1.49** (1.52; a separate agent's, read from the code at
`8c4aacc` and Angus 2.0.5's and jakarta.mail 2.1.5's bytecode, with the
suites run and the reverts this section names run again). The mechanism
holds: the budget starts at each SELECT or EXAMINE (with that restart
removed, both "each open" tests fail, run and seen), a refusal closes the
connection, and `IMAPFolder.cleanup` then drops the message cache and the
UID table; the content and header sections are told apart by the section
the client asked for, and every section Angus's `IMAPMessage`,
`IMAPNestedMessage`, `IMAPBodyPart` and `IMAPInputStream` pass, and every
`what` it passes to `fetchRFC822`, is classified right; nothing a content
fetch leaves uncharged is kept, since the folder's handler applies only the
UID, MODSEQ, flags and extension items (none, as no `FetchItem` is
registered) and no body item reaches `IMAPFolder.fetch` from it; and no
`FetchResponse` but `DepthBoundedFetchResponse` can reach `readResponse`. The
three reverts of 1.49 fail as it says, run and seen. Four statements are
narrower than written: not every body section passes `fetchSectionBody` —
`IMAPFolder.fetch` sends the batch's header fields itself, charged in full,
the safe direction; the objects are counted in more methods than B1-10's
levels and not in `readLong` or `readNumber`; the handler also creates the
message and its UID-table entry, both charged; and the session-property
tests of the connection manager and the probe fail without the property only
through Mockito's strict stubbing, since the value they assert is also the
default it falls back to.

**Reopened at 1.52** (Medium, from the same pass). The bound does not hold:
"at most some 0.86 of the budget" is true of the shapes 1.49 measured, and
shapes on the sync's own FETCH (`MessageFetcher.fetchBatch` through
`IMAPFolder.fetch`) keep far more than they are charged. Measured in process
by the pass, one hand-built response parsed through `parseFetch` and handled
as Angus handles it, with the heap read after a forced collection (rough
figures, not 1.49's method):

- **UID items, some 5×.** `IMAPFolder.fetch` gives each `UID` item of a
  response to its message, which puts it in the folder's UID table, and the
  item is read with `readLong`, which is not counted: 200,000 of them in one
  response, 2.4 MB on the wire, charged 2,400,387 bytes, made a table of
  12.8 MB, 64 bytes an entry, 5.3 times the charge. One budget of 64 MiB
  admits some 360 MB of such entries.
- **Header lines, some 25×** — B1-16 below, which is also a finding of its
  own.
- **Copies at 2–3×**, read and partly measured: a user flag with a capital
  is kept beside its lower-cased copy, so a long one costs twice its charge
  (a 100,000-character flag held 200,000 characters); the sync reads the
  subject and the personal names, which Jakarta Mail then keeps decoded as
  well; a group's address repeats its members' text; and RFC 2231 parameters
  are kept encoded and decoded.

And an account has three connections that may hold a folder open, not two: a
background move opens the destination on the source's store
(`ImapActionService.moveOnServerAsync`) while the interactive lane may hold a
third, as `MAX_MESSAGES` already says. So the residual's 2 × (58 + 67) MB is
wrong twice over; with the copy shapes alone one connection can keep about
twice the budget, and the account more than the heap.

Severity stays **Medium**, the finding's own. **Recommendation:** count what
the charge misses — one object for each `readLong` and `readNumber`, the
characters of every string read a second time (a third inside a group), and
a header section by its lines (B1-16) — then measure each shape again and
restate the ratio and the residual with three connections. And make the
session-property tests assert a value other than the default. Since the
acknowledgements in `docs/audit-freshness.json` stand at their cap of eight,
the fix needs this audit re-verified first.

**Fix (1.61, 2026-10-07, #662).** As recommended, and wider, since
measuring the shapes again through the sync's own reads found two more past
their charge. `keptBytes` now charges, besides the wire, the objects and the
response:

- every number the parse reads as an object (`readNumber`, `readLong`,
  counted in `DepthBoundedFetchResponse`), which a UID item is and nothing
  else;
- `COPIED_CHAR_BYTES` (2) for each character of every string read, a second
  copy, counted twice inside an open address group, whose address repeats its
  members' text; a flag's characters too;
- `HEADER_SET_BYTES` (1,024) for each body item, the `InternetHeaders` it is
  loaded into: the constructor without arguments holds a placeholder for some
  forty standard headers, 905 bytes (measured), before B1-16's lines;
- `STRUCTURE_PART_BYTES` (1,280) for each part of a `BODYSTRUCTURE`, the
  message's own and a nested message's included (`structureParts`). The sync
  reads every message's structure for its attachments
  (`MimePartExtractor`), and the message then keeps a data source and a
  multipart over its parts, each an `IMAPBodyPart` with a header set of its
  own: those reads added some 4.7 KB to an ordinary message of three parts
  past what its parse made (measured, 2,000 messages with and without them).

**Measured at 1.61**, retained heap after forced collections, two runs each,
in process: one response a shape (2,000 for the ordinary ones), parsed
through `parseFetch`, each item handed to a `BoundedImapMessage`'s
`handleFetchItem` as `IMAPFolder.fetch` hands it, then read as
`MessageFetcher.toFetchedMessage` reads a message (subject, addresses
formatted, dates, flags, `MimePartExtractor`'s attachment metadata, the
threading headers). Kept against charged, before the fix and after:

| Shape                                                                        | Before      | After       |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------- | ----------- |
| An ordinary message of three parts, six addresses, the sync's reads, × 2,000 | 1.32 – 1.35 | 0.70 – 0.72 |
| The same without the reads                                                   | 0.80 – 0.87 |             |
| A one-part message, × 2,000                                                  | 1.08 – 1.11 | 0.52 – 0.53 |
| 200,000 UID items in one response                                            | 6.14 – 6.22 | 0.60 – 0.61 |
| A flag of 100,000 characters with a capital                                  | 1.99        | 0.67        |
| A subject and two personal names of 30,000 encoded CJK characters each       | 1.50        | 0.50        |
| An address group of 20,000 members                                           | 0.45 – 0.46 | 0.36 – 0.37 |
| Two RFC 2231 parameters of 20,000 encoded characters                         | 1.31 – 1.72 | 0.57        |
| 200,000 header lines (B1-16)                                                 | 0.87 – 0.88 | 0.87 – 0.88 |
| An ASCII subject of 200,000 characters                                       | 1.00        | 0.33 – 0.34 |
| 20,000 empty parts                                                           | 3.44 – 3.49 | 0.79 – 0.80 |

The two shapes the pass over 1.49 did not name are the ordinary message and
the empty parts, both past their charge through the sync's reads, which
1.49's measurement did not make. After the fix the worst is 0.88, for header
lines, so one open folder keeps at most some 59 MB at the default.

**Cost.** An ordinary message is charged 16.4 KB rather than 8.7 (computed
with `keptBytes`), so a catch-up of the local window, 10,000 new messages,
is charged 164 MB and comes down over some three attempts at 64 MiB rather
than two; the flags and the UID a pass reads for each mirrored message on a
server without CONDSTORE are charged 806 bytes rather than 700, so the
floor at the defaults, the mirrored window plus one batch, is some 35 MB and
2 MB rather than 30 and 2. `OpenFolderBudgetGreenMailIT` runs at 1,664 KB
rather than 1 MB for that reason: at 1 MB every attempt stopped at the same
point, below the floor. At 1,536, 1,792 and 1,920 KB a pass whose last
attempt was refused came back clean with 355 of the 405 messages, the rest
following at the next pass; how a refusal there surfaces as a clean pass is
left to a task of its own, as it concerns the sync's handling of a lost
connection rather than the charge.

The session-property tests of the connection manager and the probe now
configure 48 MB, not the default, so a session without the property fails
their assertion rather than reading the default back: with
`BoundedImapStore.install` writing nothing, both fail on it, run and seen.

**Dynamic verification (1.61).** `BoundedImapProtocolTest.everyNumberIsAnObject`,
`stringsAreChargedACopy` (a member's characters counted twice inside a
group) and `eachStructurePartIsCharged` pin the new terms, and
`aFetchIsChargedItsObjects`, `flagsCountTwice` and `contentDataIsNotCharged`
follow them. Each term neutralized fails its tests, run and seen (below in
the change log). The whole IT suite, `HostileImapResponseIT`'s budget tests
among them, passes with the new charge.

**Residual (1.61).** The budget is per connection, and an account has
