three that may hold a folder open, each with a command's 64 MiB besides: some
3 × (59 + 67) MB together, about the packaged 384 MB heap (computed, not
measured), reached only by a server driving all three to their bounds at
once while the user acts on the account. A lower default would not close
it, since the commands' 3 × 67 MB is the larger part and the floor, the
mirrored window, is some 35 MB; one budget per account, across its folders
and its commands alike, would. **Accepted for 0.1.0 by the owner
(2026-10-07, 1.62)**: the outcome is the sidecar's out-of-memory exit, no
disclosure, and it needs a hostile server driving three connections at once
while the user acts; the per-account budget is planned after the release.
And the charge remains an estimate, an upper bound for every shape
measured; a shape that keeps more than it is charged lets a server past the
budget by that ratio.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.61, and reopened at 1.64** (a separate
agent's, read from the code at `f789efd2` and measured against it). Every
term works as built, and each neutralization 1.61 lists fails exactly the
tests it names, run and seen: numbers `aFetchIsChargedItsObjects` and
`everyNumberIsAnObject`, characters `flagsCountTwice` and
`stringsAreChargedACopy`, parts `eachStructurePartIsCharged`, the header set
`contentDataIsNotCharged`; with `BoundedImapStore.install` writing no budget,
`storeIsTheBoundedOne` and `probeUsesTheBoundedStore` fail on their
assertion, not only through strict stubbing. `OpenFolderBudgetGreenMailIT` at
1 MB logged fifteen refusals over its five passes and no pass after the
first completed, as 1.61 says. The arithmetic of 1.62 holds: 0.88 of 64 MiB
is 59.05 MB and a command's 64 MiB is 67.1 MB, so three connections are
378.5 MB, 361 MiB, 94 % of the 384 MiB heap; 1.61 and 1.62 mix MB and MiB,
which moves nothing. Three connections is right: a background move holds the
source and the destination on one store (`ImapActionService`), the
interactive lane one folder, and no other path opens two folders on a lane.

The bound does not hold. "After the fix the worst is 0.88 … so one open
folder keeps at most some 59 MB" is true of the shapes 1.61 measured, and the
pass measured two that keep more, both through the sync's read of a
message's type (`MimePartExtractor` → `isMimeType` →
`IMAPMessage.getContentType`), which renders the structure's parameters into
a string the message keeps:

- **A parameter of a long name and value, some 92 times the charge of its
  response**: B1-20, §4t, a finding of its own, since it is not a ratio but a
  growth with the product of two lengths.
- **A parameter value of 200,000 `"` characters, 1.09.** `ContentType`
  renders the value quoted, every quote escaped, 2.26 times the value; the one
  response the message keeps retained 657,960 bytes against some 603 KB
  charged (measured).

So no bound on what one folder keeps follows from the charge, and the residual
the owner accepted at 1.62 rests on a 59 MB that does not hold until both
shapes are charged. Severity stays **Medium**, the finding's own.
**Recommendation:** charge each part the length its rendered content type will
have, computed from the structure's parameters with every escape counted,
which covers both shapes (B1-20's own recommendation), and measure them
again. The residual of 1.62 holds again once the worst shape is back under
0.88.

**Corrections (1.64).** The 0.88 conclusion above is restated in the header,
in the javadocs of `keptBytes` and `DEFAULT_OPEN_FOLDER_BUDGET`, and in the
threat model; it stands corrected there by this section, and the javadocs
follow with the fix. And `OpenFolderBudgetGreenMailIT` counts its refusals by
the text `passed its budget of 1703936 bytes`, so at any other budget its first
assertion reads none; the budget is the IT's own constant, so the test is
right as long as the two move together.

**Fix (1.65, 2026-10-08, #666).** As recommended, with B1-20 (§4t): every
part's type is charged as the count `partTypeChars` gives, an upper bound of
its rendering, and a part past 16,384 drops its FETCH. Measured again, the
parameter of quotes keeps at most 0.41 of its charge, the shapes of B1-20
under the bound at most 0.61 and an RFC 2231 value 0.66 (§4t), so the worst
shape measured is 1.61's header lines at 0.88 again, and the residual the
owner accepted at 1.62 rests on its 59 MB again. The javadocs of `keptBytes`
and `DEFAULT_OPEN_FOLDER_BUDGET` say so, and `OpenFolderBudgetGreenMailIT`
names the refusal it counts by its budget constant rather than its value
written out.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.65, with a correction** (1.67; a separate
agent's, read from the code at `db05b042` and measured against it). Every part
of the fix works as built and each neutralization 1.65 lists fails the tests it
names, run and seen (§4t). What does not hold is the ratio this section and
§4t conclude: "the worst shape measured is 1.61's header lines at 0.88 again" is
true of the shapes 1.65 measured, and the pass measured one that keeps more, an
RFC 2231 parameter whose charset is long, at some 1.0 of its charge (B1-21,
§4u). It stays under the charge, so the budget still bounds what one folder
keeps; but the residual of 1.62 was computed from 0.88. At 1.0 one folder keeps
up to the budget, 67 MB, and three connections some 3 × (67 + 67) = 402 MB,
past the packaged 384 MiB (403 MB) heap rather than about it (computed). The
outcome is the same out-of-memory exit, and it still needs a hostile server
driving all three connections while the user acts. **Accepted again for 0.1.0 by
the owner (2026-10-08, 1.67)**, as B1-21; the budget per account planned after
the release closes both. The javadocs of `keptBytes` and
`DEFAULT_OPEN_FOLDER_BUDGET` still say 0.66 and 0.88, and follow with the next
change to the file.

**Status: fixed at 1.65**, confirmed by the pass over 1.65 (1.67). The honest
half (1.30, 1.37) stands; the hostile half was fixed at 1.49, reopened by its
pass at 1.52, fixed again at 1.61, reopened by its pass at 1.64, and fixed
again at 1.65. Its residual, three connections at the budget, is accepted for
0.1.0 (1.62, restated at 1.67 with B1-21).

## 4o. Finding B1-15 (Medium) — Angus's body-extension parse loops without end on an element it cannot read — **FIXED**

**What.** Found by the 1.33 verification pass, reading Angus 2.0.5's
`BODYSTRUCTURE` for B1-10. A body-extension list is parsed by
`parseBodyExtension`, which skips the `(` and then loops
`do { parseBodyExtension(r); } while (!r.isNextNonSpace(')'))`. An element
that is neither a list, a number nor a string — a bare atom such as `x` — goes
to `readString`, which returns null without consuming anything, and the next
byte is not `)`, so the loop runs again at the same index, forever. Nothing in
it reads from the socket, so no read timeout ends it, and nothing throws, so
neither `BoundedImapProtocol.readResponse` nor B1-10's count is reached.
Measured: `* 1 FETCH (UID 5 BODYSTRUCTURE ("text" "plain" NIL NIL NIL "7bit" 1 1
NIL NIL NIL (x)))` as the answer to a folder open, over TLS from
`HostileImapServer` with a throwaway test beside `HostileImapResponseIT`, left
the pass running after 20 s, in `BODYSTRUCTURE.parseBodyExtension`, using
1.94 s of CPU in 2 s of wall time; a second pass for the account, against an
ordinary answer, returned within milliseconds having recorded nothing, and the
test JVM had to be killed at its end. The same line through `parseFetch` alone
did not return within 5 s. Read, not measured: the spinning thread is the
account's sync, holding its connection, so the account is not synced again
until the process ends, and a restart meets the same response.

A sender cannot produce it: a conforming server writes extension data as
strings, numbers and lists, so a bare atom there takes a hostile or
compromised server, as the shapes of 1.31 did. A response that ends inside an
extension list instead reads past the buffer and throws, which
`readResponse` already handles by closing the connection.

**Severity: Medium** (_DoS_), on B1-10's reasoning: automatic sync, no user
interaction, and it outlasts a restart; it also holds one CPU core for as long
as the process runs.

**Recommendation.** Stop a parse that stops consuming. `DepthBoundedFetchResponse`
already sees every call Angus makes through `readByte`, `skip`,
`isNextNonSpace` and `readString`; a bound on how many of them one response may
take — a small multiple of its length, since an honest parse consumes at least
a byte for every few calls — or a refusal of the same index seen twice by
`isNextNonSpace(')')` without anything consumed in between, would end it
through the same `NestingLimitReached` path and drop the response. Which, and
its measured margin over an honest parse, is a decision at fix time; the version pin of `angusIsTheVerifiedVersion` already ties the reading to 2.0.5.

**Fix (shipped 2026-10-05, 1.35).** The second of the two, generalized:
every method `DepthBoundedFetchResponse` overrides — `readByte`, `skip`,
`isNextNonSpace`, `readString` — notes after the call whether the index has
moved since the last one, and `MAX_STALLED_CALLS` (64) calls in a row that
did not move it stop the parse with `ParseStalled`. `parseFetch` turns that
into `StalledParseException`, a `ProtocolException` like B1-10's
`NestedTooDeepException`, so the response, read in full, is dropped and the
connection stays in step; the message it describes takes the envelope-only
stub. The loop of this finding calls `readString` and `isNextNonSpace` at
one index and is stopped within 64 calls. An honest parse makes a few such
calls in a row at most — an `isNextNonSpace` that finds no `)` before the
read that consumes the next element — so the margin is wide. Not a bound on
calls per response, the other option: that would have to be sized against the
longest honest parse, and the stall needs no such measurement. A loop that
moved the index could not run for ever either, the response being finite.

**Dynamic verification.** `BoundedImapProtocolTest` parses the shape of this
finding in both branches that reach `parseBodyExtension`, a single part's
extension data and a multipart's, under a ten-second limit: each is dropped
as stalled. With the stall check disabled both time out, which was run and
seen; the parse has no other way to end. `HostileImapResponseIT` sends the
single-part shape as the answer to a folder open over TLS: the pass completes
and records nothing. Its failure without the fix is the 1.33 measurement
above, not repeated: the spinning thread cannot be stopped, and it held the
test JVM open.

**Residual.** None beyond B1-10's: a message whose structure the parse stops
on is listed by its envelope and cannot be opened.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.37** (#635), from Angus 2.0.5's source: the body-extension loop is the one parse loop that can spin, and it runs through `readString` and `isNextNonSpace`, both counted; every other loop — through `readSimpleList`, `skipSpaces`, a literal's skip, the address-group loop — is bounded by the response's size or throws. An honest parse makes one to three calls in a row without consuming, since every successful read moves the index. Measured through `parseFetch`, the shape of this finding is dropped as stalled in about a tenth of a second.

**Status: fixed at 1.35, confirmed by the pass over 1.37.**

## 4p. Finding B1-16 (Medium) — a header section is kept at some 25 times its charge, one response at a time — **FIXED**

**What.** Found by the pass over 1.49 (1.52). A header section is one byte
array in the FETCH parse, so the charges see one object and its bytes, but
Jakarta Mail's `InternetHeaders.load` makes each of its lines an
`InternetHeader` with a name and a line `String`, some 126 bytes for a
five-byte line `a:b`. The sync's own batch asks every message for three
header fields (`MessageFetcher.fetchBatch`, `BODY.PEEK[HEADER.FIELDS
(Message-ID In-Reply-To References)]` through `IMAPFolder.fetch`), and
`IMAPMessage.handleFetchItem` loads the answer into the message's headers,
kept until the folder closes; `IMAPMessage.getHeader` and `loadHeaders` and
`IMAPBodyPart.loadHeaders` do the same through `fetchSectionBody`, which
charges them as headers, once. Measured in process by the pass: 200,000 such
lines, 1,000,081 bytes on the wire and charged 1,000,649 by `keptBytes`,
became headers holding some 25.2 MB, 25 times the charge (read after a
forced collection; rough).

**Why it is a finding of its own.** The headers are built after every check
has admitted the response: one response inside B1-7's 32 MiB is some 800 MB
once loaded, by the measured ratio, past the packaged heap in one response,
so neither B1-7's bound nor B1-8's nor B1-14's stops it. The
`-XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError` exit follows, and the restart meets the same
folder on the next cycle.

**Severity: Medium** (DoS), on B1-3's reasoning: automatic sync, a hostile
or compromised server, the out-of-memory exit, and a restart meets the same
folder.

**Recommendation.** Charge a header section by its lines: for each body item
of a fetch that is not content, count the line breaks in its data and charge
some 128 bytes for each, in `keptBytes`, so the response is refused in
`readResponse` before Angus loads it; or refuse a header section with more
lines than an honest one carries. Measure the ratio again after.

**Fix (1.56, 2026-10-07, #657).** As recommended, by charging the lines.
`keptBytes` charges every body item it does not leave to a fetch of content
as the headers it becomes (`BoundedImapProtocol.headerBytes`):
`HEADER_LINE_BYTES` (128) for each header, and the header block's bytes
twice more, for the line and the name each header keeps as strings. Every
body item, not only a header section: a folder's handler loads a header
section into the message's headers and parses anything else with
`MimeMessage.parse`, which loads the headers at its start the same way. The
headers are counted as `InternetHeaders.load` makes them (`headerBlock`):
one for each line before the first empty one that does not start with a space
or a tab, the first line whatever it starts with, and the lines split as
`LineInputStream` splits them, at an LF, a CR LF, a CR CR LF or a CR alone,
since a line end it splits at and the count did not would hide a header, and
a CR CR LF taken for two would end the block early. The charge goes to the
selection budget in `readResponse`, so a response whose headers would pass
it is refused there, with the connection, before Angus loads them. Measured
against Jakarta Mail 2.1.5, the heap `InternetHeaders` retains against the
charge of the whole FETCH: 0.90 for 200,000 lines `a:b`, 0.95 with each line
ended by a CR alone, 0.89 by a CR CR LF, 0.68 for 1,000-character names with
a colon, 0.35 without one, 0.60 for long values and 0.61 for folded lines. An
ordinary message's FETCH (§4n's five addresses, three-part structure and
three threading headers) is charged 9.7 KB rather than 8.8, so a catch-up of
`local-window-limit` new messages 97 MB rather than 88. This closes the
header-line shape of B1-14's reopening (§4n); the others stand.

**Dynamic verification (1.56).** `BoundedImapProtocolTest`:
`aHeaderSectionIsChargedAsItsHeaders` charges a section of 500,000 lines
`a:b`, 2.5 MB on the wire, past the default budget;
`headersAreCountedAsJakartaMailMakesThem` counts eleven blocks — every line
end, folded lines, a folded first line, an empty first line, no colon, no
line end, nothing — exactly as `InternetHeaders` makes them; and
`theHeaderBlockEndsAtTheFirstEmptyLine` checks the block's end and a folded
line. `contentDataIsNotCharged` now also charges a body item outside a
content fetch its headers. With the header charge taken out,
`aHeaderSectionIsChargedAsItsHeaders` and `contentDataIsNotCharged` fail;
with lines split at an LF alone, a CR CR LF read as two ends, or folded lines
counted as headers, the count against Jakarta Mail fails, and with the first
and last the block test too; all run and seen. `HostileImapResponseIT` and
`OpenFolderBudgetGreenMailIT` pass unchanged.

**Residual.** The charge is an estimate of Jakarta Mail 2.1.5's
`InternetHeaders`, as `PARSED_OBJECT_BYTES` is of Angus 2.0.5, measured
rather than derived; a later version may keep more per header. A whitespace
first line is counted as a header that `load` does not make, the one place
the count is high.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.56** (1.57; the same agent's, at `05e8ca4`).
`keptBytes` charges `headerBytes` for every `BODY` and `RFC822DATA` item
outside a content fetch, and `readResponse` applies the charge before Angus is
handed the response. Every path that builds `InternetHeaders` from a FETCH
reaches it: `IMAPFolder.fetch` through `handleFetchItem`; `getHeader`,
`loadHeaders`, `IMAPBodyPart.loadHeaders` and a nested message's headers
through `fetchSectionBody` with a section that is not content; `fetchRFC822`
with `HEADER`. Nothing else takes a body item from a response:
`IMAPFolder.handleResponse` and `IMAPMessage.fetchItem` take none, and the
`readEach` responses reach no handler (read). The line splitting matches
`LineInputStream.readLine` for every end the pass tried, a CR CR and another
byte and a CR at the end of the data among them, and no
`mail.mime.allowutf8` is set, so the strings are Latin-1. Each neutralization
1.56 lists fails the tests it names, run and seen. Remeasured, 200,000 lines
each, what the headers retain against the charge (rough): 0.913 for `a:b`
ended by CR LF, 0.932 for a CR alone and for an LF alone, 0.966 for `a:`
ended by an LF and 0.611 for `a`. The bound holds; 1.56's "at most 0.95" and
its 0.95 for a CR alone were narrower than measured here. Two shapes outside
the budget are findings of their own: the untouched send parses the server's
draft with no charge (B1-18, §4r), and Angus merges a second header item into
a message's headers at a cost that grows with the square of their count
(B1-19, §4s). The Residual is accurate as far as the count goes.

**Status: fixed at 1.56**, confirmed by the pass over 1.56 (1.57). Found by
the pass over 1.49 (1.52).

## 4q. Finding B1-17 (Medium) — nested address groups repeat their members' text at each level, inside the parse — **FIXED**

**What.** Found by the pass over 1.49 (1.52), from Angus 2.0.5's bytecode
(`IMAPAddress`, offsets 102–240) and measured in process. For a group's
start Angus sets the group's address to its name, a colon, the `toString()`
of every member and a semicolon, and keeps the member list besides; a nested
group's `toString()` is its own such address, so each level holds the whole
subtree's text again, and what an envelope keeps grows with the depth times
its innermost text. Measured through `parseFetch` with a 1,000-character
personal name innermost: 50 levels, 1,937 bytes on the wire, held 55,638
characters, charged 12,585 bytes; 200 levels, 4,487 bytes, held 264,513
characters, charged 43,935. B1-10 admits groups nested some 250 deep, so an
innermost name of some 1.5 MiB, as a literal, comes to some 375 MB in one
response (by the measured growth, not measured at that size).

**Why it is a finding of its own.** The strings are built inside Angus's
parse, inside `readResponse`, before the response exists for any of the
per-response, per-command or per-folder checks to refuse it. It is the
envelope of every sync batch, and the same shape inside a `message/rfc822`
part's structure. RFC 5322 §3.4 allows no group inside a group, and §4j
already calls the shape non-conforming.

**Severity: Medium** (DoS), on the same reasoning as B1-16.

**Recommendation.** In `DepthBoundedFetchResponse`, drop a FETCH whose
address list starts a group while another group of the same list is still
open, as a response nested too deep is dropped (B1-10): the class already
tracks where it is in an address and whether its host is NIL, and needs the
mailbox's NIL-ness besides, to tell a group's start from its end, and a count
of open groups per list.

**Fix (1.55, 2026-10-07, #656).** As recommended. `DepthBoundedFetchResponse`
reads an address's mailbox as well as its host, so a counted address, one
with a NIL host, starts a group when its mailbox is there and ends one when
it is NIL, and it counts the groups started and not ended in the list being
read. A group that starts while another is open stops the parse with
`NestedGroup` at the inner group's `)`, before Angus reads its members or
builds any group's text, and `parseFetch` drops the response with
`NestedGroupException`, a `ProtocolException` like B1-10's, logged as a group
inside another. The open groups are forgotten with the list, as B1-10's group
markers are, so a group a list leaves open, which Angus ends with the list,
does not carry into the next one, and a stray end at the top of a list, which
Angus ignores, ends nothing. With no group starting inside another, Angus's
group parse recurses one level at most; B1-10's count of group markers is
unchanged.

**Dynamic verification (1.55).** `BoundedImapProtocolTest.aGroupInsideAGroupIsDropped`
drops two levels, the 200 levels around a 1,000-character name that 1.52
measured, and a second group started before the first ends;
`groupsThatDoNotNestParse` parses groups one after another, a stray end, and a
group left open in two lists in a row; `nestedAddressGroupsAreDropped` now
meets the new drop before B1-10's bound. With the drop neutralized,
`aGroupInsideAGroupIsDropped` and `nestedAddressGroupsAreDropped` fail, the
latter dropped at B1-10's bound instead; with the open groups not forgotten
with their list, `groupsThatDoNotNestParse` fails; with every counted address
taken for a group's start, `ordinaryGroupsParse`,
`groupsInSeparateEnvelopesDoNotAddUp` and `groupsThatDoNotNestParse` fail; all
run and seen. `HostileImapResponseIT.addressGroupsNestedPastTheStackAreDropped`
syncs from a server that nests 100,000 groups, and the pass goes on, now
through the new drop.

**Residual.** Groups one after another are bounded by B1-10's count, and each
holds its members' text once, as an honest group does. A FETCH carrying a
group inside a group is dropped whole, as B1-10 drops one nested too deep,
though RFC 5322 has no such shape for an honest server to send.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.55** (1.57; the same agent's, at `05e8ca4`).
The mailbox's and the host's NIL-ness are read from the very values Angus's
`readString` returns, and `IMAPAddress` decides a group's start and end from
the same two, so whatever Angus reads as NIL — NIL itself, an unquoted atom
starting with N, any other atom, an unterminated quoted string, a literal
with a bad count — the check reads as NIL too. A `FetchResponse` is built
only in `IMAPProtocol.readResponse`, which `BoundedImapProtocol` overrides, so
the greeting and the uncharged `readEach` responses go through the check as
well, and the one address parser is the envelope's, reached from a FETCH
ENVELOPE and from a `message/rfc822` part's structure. No honest structure
reaches a counted marker: a parameter list reaches the third string at most,
and a disposition, an rfc822 envelope and body extensions reset the stage
(read). Nothing in the backend calls `getGroup()` or `isGroup()`, so no other
parser repeats a group's text. Each neutralization 1.55 lists fails the tests
it names, run and seen; with the drop disabled `HostileImapResponseIT` still
passes, its groups dropped at B1-10's bound instead. No route around the fix
was found, and the Residual is accurate.

**Status: fixed at 1.55**, confirmed by the pass over 1.55 (1.57). Found by
the pass over 1.49 (1.52).

## 4r. Finding B1-18 (Medium) — sending an untouched draft parses the server's header block uncharged — **FIXED**

**What.** Found by the pass over 1.56 (1.57); measured. The untouched send
reads the server's draft through `ImapAppendService.fetchAndDetachMime`, a
content fetch, which the selection budget leaves uncharged as B1-14 means it
to, into a buffer bounded at `MAX_DRAFT_BYTES` (40 MiB, §4e), and then builds
`new MimeMessage(session, stream)` from it, which loads the whole header block
into `InternetHeaders` before the recipient check runs: B1-16's shape on a
path its charge does not reach. Measured through the real service (rough): an
8 MiB draft of 1,677,720 headers retained 212 MiB, 26.6 times its size, and a
40 MiB one, 41,943,038 bytes and so within the bound, 8,388,606 headers and
1,046 MiB, past the packaged 384 MB heap.

**Severity: Medium** (DoS), on B1-12's reasoning: the user's own action, the
send of a draft left untouched, on data the server chooses, ending in the
out-of-memory exit; the server can serve the draft's opening normally.

**Recommendation.** Bound the header block before the parse: count it with
`BoundedImapProtocol.headerBlock` in `fetchAndDetachMime` and refuse a draft
whose headers would keep more than an honest draft comes near, or charge them
against `MAX_DRAFT_BYTES` the way `headerBytes` charges them against the
budget.

**Fix (1.59, 2026-10-07, #660).** As recommended, by the first way, and
wider than recommended, since the top-level block is not the only one the send
parses. `fetchAndDetachMime` charges the draft's header block on the bytes as
held, before anything parses them, as `BoundedImapProtocol.headerBytes`
charges a body item (`HEADER_LINE_BYTES` a header and the block's bytes twice
more, through a new overload on a byte array), and refuses a draft whose block
would cost more than `MAX_DRAFT_HEADER_BYTES` (256 KiB): a block of 128 KiB,
some three thousand recipients of 40 bytes. The address parse of the
recipient check then keeps some 76 bytes an address at its densest
(`a,a,a…`, 262,143 addresses in a 512 KiB `To` retained some 19 MB,
measured), so near 5 MB at the bound. And the send no longer parses the
content at all. `MimeMessage.saveChanges` marks a message modified and runs
`MimeBodyPart.updateHeaders`, which takes a multipart content through
`DataHandler.getContent` and `MimeMultipart.updateHeaders`, parsing every
part's header block into `InternetHeaders` and every nested multipart the same
way (read from the bytecode of Jakarta Mail 2.1.5); the top-level bound
charges none of those blocks, and a draft of 5 MB with a million header lines
in its second part ran a 64 MB heap out of memory there (measured, in
process). The draft is now an `ImapAppendService.UntouchedDraft`, whose
`saveChanges` updates the top-level `MIME-Version`, `Date` and `Message-ID`
only and leaves the message unmodified, so `writeTo` writes the headers and
copies the content as held. That is what went out before as well: the parts
were updated on a multipart that saving then dropped, and an unmodified
`MimePartDataHandler` wrote the content raw (measured on three drafts, the
bytes the same either way). What changes is that nothing parses the content,
so a multipart whose boundary never appears, which that parse refused with
`Missing start boundary`, now goes out as the server holds it. Nothing else on
the send path reads the content: `SMTPTransport.sendMessage` calls
`convertTo8Bit` and a second `saveChanges` only under `allow8bitmime`, which
the application never sets, its `mailFrom` reads `getFrom`, and the Sent
append's `MessageLiteral` calls `writeTo`, `getSize`, `getFlags` and the dates
(read from the bytecode of Angus 2.0.5).

**Dynamic verification (1.59).** `ImapAppendServiceTest.headerBlockPastTheBoundIsRefused`
serves a draft of one short header past the bound and expects the refusal,
and `headerBlockWithinTheBoundIsParsed` one at it, which parses with every
header; `savingAnUntouchedDraftParsesNoPart` gives the draft's second part
200,000 header lines and measures what saving and writing it allocate on the
test's thread: 0.94 MB, against 38.6 MB with the draft a plain `MimeMessage`
(both measured), the bound in the test 4 MB. With the charge skipped,
`headerBlockPastTheBoundIsRefused` fails, and with the plain `MimeMessage` in
place of `UntouchedDraft`, `savingAnUntouchedDraftParsesNoPart` fails; each
neutralization fails that test alone, run and seen.
`untouchedDraftSendsItsContentAsHeld` checks the content goes out byte for
byte and the top-level headers are updated as every send updated them; it
passes with the plain `MimeMessage` too, since that path sent the same bytes.

**Residual (1.59).** The draft is still held twice, as the bytes read off
the wire and the message's copy of its content, within `MAX_DRAFT_BYTES` each
(§4e). A draft whose header block costs more than 256 KiB cannot be sent
untouched, however honest; it can be opened and sent from the composer, which
builds the message from what the user sees.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.59** (1.64; the same agent's, at
`f789efd2`). The block is charged before anything parses it, through the byte
array overload of `headerBytes`, and nothing else on the send path reads the
content: `allow8bitmime` comes from the session's properties only and nothing
sets it, the Sent append calls `writeTo`, `getSize`, `getFlags` and the dates,
and `MimeMessage.parse` copies the content into an array of its own (read in
the bytecode of Angus 2.0.5 and Jakarta Mail 2.1.5). Each neutralization 1.59
lists fails the one test it names, run and seen; with the plain `MimeMessage`
the test measured 37.5 MB where 1.59 wrote 38.6, against its 4 MB bound. The
address parse at its densest, two runs: 262,143 addresses in a To of
524 KB retained 19,922,888 bytes, 76.0 an address (1.59's "some 19 MB"), and
65,500 near the bound 4.98 MB. No route around was found.

**Corrections (1.64).** The Residual's "held twice … within
`MAX_DRAFT_BYTES` each" is short of the peak. The bytes are read into a
`ByteArrayOutputStream`, whose array doubles as it grows, so a draft between
32 and 40 MiB leaves a 64 MiB array, and it is held beside the content's copy
of up to 40 MiB while the `UntouchedDraft` is built: some 104 MiB at the peak
(measured by the pass). The javadoc of `MAX_DRAFT_BYTES` says 80 MB at its
worst and is short by the same 24 MiB; it follows with the next change to the
file. Neither is a bound that fails: the peak is on the user's own send, once.

**Status: fixed at 1.59**, confirmed by the pass over 1.59 (1.64). Found by
the pass over 1.56 (1.57).

## 4s. Finding B1-19 (Medium) — merging a second header item costs the square of its headers — **FIXED**

**What.** Found by the pass over 1.56 (1.57); measured in process. When a
message already has headers and the fetch did not ask for all of them, as in
the sync's `fetchBatch`, `IMAPMessage.handleFetchItem` merges a header item
into them with one `InternetHeaders.addHeader` per header, and Jakarta Mail
2.1.5's `addHeader` scans the whole list backwards with `equalsIgnoreCase`
before it inserts, so the merge costs the square of the number of headers
with distinct names. `IMAPFolder.fetch` hands every item of every FETCH
response of the command to the handler, so two header items for one message
are enough: the first sets the headers, the second is merged. B1-16's charge
admits some 416,800 headers of 161 bytes under the 64 MiB budget. Measured
through the real `handleFetchItem`, with the items parsed and charged by
`BoundedImapProtocol`: 20,000 headers in the second item took 1.2 s, 40,000
took 11.1 s, 80,000 took 52.3 s and 160,000, charged 25.8 MB, took 163 s;
80,000 with 43-character names took 73.9 s. At the budget's limit, by the
same growth, some 18 minutes a response (not measured), on the sync's thread
with the account's lane held, every cycle. Not driven end to end over a
server.

**Severity: Medium** (DoS), on B1-13's reasoning: time rather than memory, on
the automatic sync, every cycle.

**Recommendation.** Cap the headers of one body item outside a content fetch
at what an honest answer carries, some hundreds, refusing the response past
it in `readResponse`; or refuse a second header item for one message within a
command.

**Fix (1.60, 2026-10-07, #661).** By neither way, since neither bounds what
adds up. A message keeps its headers for as long as the folder is open, and
`IMAPFolder.fetch` hands it every item of every FETCH response of the
command, so a server can merge into one message item after item: within one
command, in each of a folder's commands, and under a fresh number after an
EXPUNGE. A cap on each item leaves the items many, and the merges add up to
the square of their sum whatever each carries; by the open-folder budget
alone, some B²/(2 × 138²) comparisons, 10¹¹ for 64 MiB (estimated, not
measured). Refusing a second item within a command leaves the commands and
the renumbering. What adds up is the merges into one message, so they are
counted on the message. `BoundedImapStore` makes every folder a
`BoundedImapFolder` (both forms of `newIMAPFolder` Angus calls; the
`mail.<protocol>.folder.class` its own reads is never set), whose
`newIMAPMessage` makes a `BoundedImapMessage`. Its `handleFetchItem` counts
the header lines of every item Angus would merge, with
`BoundedImapProtocol.headerBlock`: a header item, for a fetch that did not ask
for all headers, into a message that holds headers already, the condition
Angus's merge takes (read from Angus 2.0.5's bytecode). An item that would
take the message past `MAX_MERGED_HEADER_LINES` (256) over its life is not
handed to Angus. The message keeps what it holds, a header it was asked for
and did not get is loaded whole when it is read (`IMAPMessage.loadHeaders`),
and the folder logs one line. A merge then costs at most 256 times the
headers the message holds, each charged to the open-folder budget when it
arrived (B1-16), so a folder's open spends at most some 2 × 256 × 486,000
comparisons merging, the budget's 64 MiB at 138 bytes a short header: some
2.5 × 10⁸ (estimated, not measured). The sync asks for three headers, and an
honest message merges a handful.

**Dynamic verification (1.60).** `HostileImapResponseIT.aHeaderItemPastTheMergeBoundIsSkipped`
goes through the real `IMAPFolder.fetch` over the test server. It sends two
FETCH answers for one message, the second of 80,000 headers whose names have
one length, within the IT's 16 MiB budget. The fetch ends in 0.03 s and the
message keeps its first `Message-ID`. With the bound removed it ran into its
time limit (15 s then, 5 s now), and run to its end it took 18.5 s on the
desktop that wrote this, where the pass measured 52 s; all measured.
`BoundedImapMessageTest.mergesStopAtTheBound` merges up to the bound across
items and refuses the item past it, and fails with the bound removed, run and
seen. `aHeaderItemMergesIntoTheHeadersHeld` and `theFirstLoadIsNotCounted`
pin that an ordinary merge still happens and that the headers a message first
loads, at a cost that grows with their size alone, are not counted against
it.

**Residual (1.60).** A folder's open can still spend the estimated 2.5 × 10⁸
comparisons merging, some seconds at the worst, once per open. A message past
its bound reads a header it was asked for with a fetch of its whole header
block, charged like any other.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.60** (1.64; the same agent's, at
`f789efd2`). Every folder the app opens is a `BoundedImapFolder`: the store
overrides both forms of `newIMAPFolder` Angus calls, nothing sets
`mail.<protocol>.folder.class`, and messages are made through
`newIMAPMessage` (`MessageCache` and `IMAPFolder`); the `DefaultFolder` Angus
builds directly holds no messages. Every store is bounded, enumerated with
`rg -n 'getStore\(|new IMAPStore|new IMAPSSLStore|BoundedImapStore.install' backend/src/main`:
the stores are made only in `ImapConnectionManager` and
`MailConnectionProbe`, each after `install`, and `ImapActionService` and
`MailSyncService` reuse a store they are given. The merge condition is
Angus's, `IMAPFolder.fetch` is its only caller, and `processFetchResponse`
applies only UID, MODSEQ and FLAGS. The estimate holds: the cheapest charged
header is some 134 bytes, which gives 2.56 × 10⁸ rather than 2.49. With the
bound removed `mergesStopAtTheBound` fails and the IT runs into its 5 s limit,
run and seen. No route around was found.

**Corrections (1.64).** "A header it was asked for and did not get is
loaded whole when it is read (`IMAPMessage.loadHeaders`)", in the Fix above,
in the Residual and in `BoundedImapMessage`'s javadoc, is wrong about the
method: `IMAPMessage.getHeader(name)` on a message that lacks the header
fetches `BODY.PEEK[HEADER.FIELDS (name)]` alone and appends what comes back
with `InternetHeaders.load`, which costs in proportion to its size and is
charged as a header section (read from Angus 2.0.5). No security effect: that
load is linear, as is `IMAPBodyPart.loadHeaders`, five `addHeader` calls a
part. The javadoc follows with the next change to the file.

**Status: fixed at 1.60**, confirmed by the pass over 1.60 (1.64). Found by
the pass over 1.56 (1.57).

## 4t. Finding B1-20 (Medium) — a structure parameter is rendered at a size that grows with the product of its name and value, outside every charge — **FIXED**

**What.** Found by the pass over 1.61 (1.64); measured. The sync reads
every message's type for its attachments (`MimePartExtractor` → `isMimeType`
→ `IMAPMessage.getContentType`), and Angus renders it from the structure, as
`new ContentType(type, subtype, cParams).toString()`, and keeps the string on
the message; every `IMAPBodyPart` does the same in its constructor (read from
Angus 2.0.5). Both live as long as the folder is open. Jakarta Mail 2.1.5's
`ParameterList.toString` splits a value longer than 60 characters into
segments of 60 under `mail.mime.splitlongparameters`, on by default, and
writes the parameter's name again in each (`name*0*=…; name*1*=…`, read from
the bytecode), so the string is some (V / 60) × (K + 70) characters for a
name of K and a value of V: the two lengths multiply. A name and a value of
10,000 characters each render to 1,681,573 characters and of 30,000 each to
15,034,903 (measured, standalone). The charge (§4n) counts the parameter's
characters as read, not the rendering. Through the real path — the test
server (`HostileImapServer`), `BoundedImapStore`, `IMAPFolder.fetch` and
`MessageFetcher.fetchBatch` — one message whose `BODYSTRUCTURE` is
`("application" "x" ({30000}n… {30000}v…) NIL NIL "7bit" 0)`, a line of
60 KB, retained 16.90 MB and 16.84 MB in two runs (after four forced
collections) against 549,018 bytes charged for the whole open, three equal
responses: some 92 times the charge of one response, 30.7 times the
open's. Nothing bounds the two lengths but the 32 MiB a response and the
budget, so one admitted response, or a few messages of one batch, can pass
the 384 MB heap (computed, not run); the batch is then not stored, and the
next cycle fetches the same message again.

**Severity: Medium** (DoS), on B1-16's and B1-17's reasoning: the automatic
sync, every cycle, ending in the out-of-memory exit
(`-XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError`), the restarted sidecar meeting the same
message. Whether an honest server relays such a parameter from a sender's
mail was not measured; a name that long does not fit a line of RFC 5322, so
by that route the rendering would grow with the value alone, some 17 times.

**Recommendation.** Drop the FETCH, as B1-17 does, when a structure
parameter's name is longer than a bound well above an honest name (honest
names stay under some 40 characters; 256 would do). And charge each part, in
`keptBytes`, the length its rendered content type will have, computed from
the parameters with an escaped character counted as two, which also covers the
parameter of quotes that keeps 1.09 of its charge (§4n). Turning
`mail.mime.splitlongparameters` off would remove the multiplication, but it is
a JVM-wide property and changes how outgoing file names are written, and was
not tried. A `HostileImapResponseIT` case should sync the shape above and
refuse it.

**Fix (1.65, 2026-10-08, #666).** By the recommendation's second way, and
by a bound on the rendering rather than on the name, since a name within any
honest bound still multiplies: at 256 characters the type is some 4.6 times
the value. `BoundedImapProtocol.partTypeChars` counts, from the parsed
structure, the most a part's type can render to, as Jakarta Mail 2.1.5's
`ParameterList.toString` writes a value it holds as a plain string, which a
parameter the server named plainly is (read from the bytecode): a value past 60
characters in segments of 60, each with the name again and 20 characters for
the separator, a fold, the segment's number, the `=` and the quotes; a quote, a
backslash, a CR or an LF escaped; a space or a tab possibly preceded by a
fold's CR LF; and a CR or an LF possibly followed by the space
`MimeUtility.makesafe` adds. Two uses:

- `parseFetch` drops a FETCH whose structure has a part past `MAX_TYPE_CHARS`
  (16,384) with `OversizedTypeException`, a ProtocolException like B1-17's,
  once the response is parsed and before anything renders it: the message is
  left without its structure, and the sync goes on. An honest type counts far
  under it: a 255-character file name with a charset, some 400.
- `keptBytes` charges every part's count as another copy, at
  `COPIED_CHAR_BYTES` a character (`typeChars`), so what passes the drop is
  inside the budget.

The split's own time, which copies the rest of the value at each segment and
so grows the same way, is bounded by the drop too. A value the server encoded
by RFC 2231 is written as it came, unsplit, and segments the server sent are
written one by one as they came: both are linear in the wire, which the wire
bytes and the copy charge already pay for, and the count sees only their
decoded value, which it counts all the same. The disposition's parameters are
not rendered on the sync's path (`IMAPBodyPart` and `IMAPMessage` read them by
name), so they are not counted.

**Measured at 1.65**, retained heap after forced collections, two runs each,
in process: a shape's FETCH parsed through `parseFetch`, its structure kept and
every part's type rendered as Angus renders it, 200 responses a shape (2,000
for the ordinary structure). The parts Angus makes of a structure are not
built here; `STRUCTURE_PART_BYTES` charges them (1.61). Kept against charged:

| Shape (largest under the bound)                                   | Count  | Rendered | Kept / charged |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ------ | -------- | -------------- |
| A name of 100 over a value of 5,000                               | 15,093 | 14,159   | 0.43 – 0.61    |
| A name of 1 over a value of 10,000                                | 13,520 | 11,740   | 0.37 – 0.38    |
| A value of 6,000 quotes                                           | 14,113 | 13,203   | 0.41           |
| An RFC 2231 value of 1,500 euro signs, 13,500 encoded characters  | 2,038  | 13,528   | 0.66           |
| 150 segments of 60 the server sent                                | 12,163 | 10,553   | 0.44           |
| A three-part structure with a charset, a file name and a boundary | 252    |          | 0.37 – 0.49    |

The name and value of 30,000 each the pass measured are dropped. So the
worst shape measured stays 1.61's header lines at 0.88. The charge an
ordinary message costs grows by its types, 504 bytes for the three-part
structure above, from some 16.4 KB to 16.9 (computed), and a catch-up of the
local window from 164 MB to some 169; it still comes down in some three
attempts at 64 MiB.

**Dynamic verification (1.65).** `BoundedImapProtocolTest`
`aTypePastTheBoundIsDropped` drops a 2,000-character name over a
2,000-character value as the part, as a part of a multipart and as the
multipart's own parameters, and a value of 8,000 quotes;
`anHonestTypeParses` parses the 255-character file name and counts it between
300 and 500; `theTypeCountBoundsTheRendering` checks the count against
`ContentType.toString` itself for fifteen values, empty, at 60 and 61, long,
of quotes, backslashes, spaces, tabs, CR LF, CR, LF, a mix and a non-Latin-1
character, under names of 1, 8 and 300 characters, and 500 parameters;
`eachStructurePartIsCharged` now expects the types' count in the charge.
`HostileImapResponseIT.aTypePastTheBoundIsDropped` serves the pass's own
shape over the test server and expects the drop in the log and a clean pass.
Neutralized, each run and seen to fail: the drop (the bound set to
`Integer.MAX_VALUE`) fails `aTypePastTheBoundIsDropped` and the IT; the charge
fails `eachStructurePartIsCharged`; one segment a value, or no segment
characters, fails `aTypePastTheBoundIsDropped` and
`theTypeCountBoundsTheRendering`; a quote counted once fails both; a CR or an
LF counted once or twice fails `theTypeCountBoundsTheRendering`. A space or a
tab counted once fails nothing, since the segment characters leave room for
the folds a segment can take; the three is kept as the bound the reading
gives.

**Residual (1.65).** A server can still make a part's type render to up to
16,384 counted characters, charged; and a message whose structure it inflates
past that loses its structure, as one dropped by B1-10 or B1-17 does: it is
listed by its envelope, without attachments. An honest type would have to
carry a parameter of thousands of characters to meet the bound; none is
known.

**Confirmed by the pass over 1.65, with corrections** (1.67; a separate
agent's, read from the code at `db05b042` and from Angus 2.0.5's and Jakarta
Mail 2.1.5's bytecode, measured against it). The type is rendered only in
`IMAPMessage.getContentType`, whose cached field `getDataHandler` reuses, and in
the constructor of `IMAPBodyPart`; the disposition's parameters are read by
name and never rendered. Every FETCH goes through `parseFetch`, the ones
`readEach` streams included, so they meet the drop as well; there the dropped
response is skipped by the stream's own `catch (ProtocolException)` and the
command goes on. A message whose structure is dropped, on the batch's own FETCH
included, is stored as an envelope-only stub. No plainly named value was found
whose rendering exceeds the count, beyond the fifteen shapes the test pins. The
measured table reproduces within its ranges. Each neutralization 1.65 lists
fails the tests it names, run and seen. Corrections: a CR or an LF needs a
weight of 4, not 5 (at 1, 2 and 3 `theTypeCountBoundsTheRendering` fails, at 4
it passes), and a space or a tab needs no more than 1 on the shapes tested,
since the segment characters absorb a segment's folds; both weights are kept as
the reading gives them, an upper bound with room. The count bounds the
rendering only for plainly named values; one the server encoded by RFC 2231, or
sent in segments, renders as long as it came and is counted by its decoded
value, which a long charset leaves far shorter: such a value is not dropped,
and is paid for by the wire and copy charges, at the ratio B1-21 gives (§4u).
The 504 bytes for an ordinary structure are computed for the structure in the
table, a longer file name and boundary than the pass's own three parts, which
counted 376.

**Status: fixed at 1.65**, confirmed by the pass over 1.65 (1.67). Found by the
pass over 1.61 (1.64).

## 4u. Finding B1-21 (Low) — an open folder can keep some 1.0 of its charge, not 0.88 — **ACCEPTED**

**What.** Found by the pass over 1.65 (1.67); measured. A structure parameter
the server encodes by RFC 2231 with a long charset, `n*` set to K characters of
charset, two quotes and `%41%41`, decodes to two characters, so
`partTypeChars` counts some 40 and the part is not dropped, rightly, since its
rendering grows with its wire length and not past it. But Jakarta Mail keeps the
value's encoded form and its charset, each some K characters, and the rendered
type repeats the encoded form: three copies of what the wire carried once, all
compact Latin-1 strings, against the wire bytes and a copy at two bytes a
character. Kept against charged, counted string by string and corroborated by
the retained heap, two runs each: 0.53 at K = 1,000, 0.92 at 10,000, 0.99 at
100,000 and 1.00 at a million and five million. It approaches the charge from
below and does not pass it, so the budget still bounds what a folder keeps;
it is past the 0.88 that §4n and §4t concluded and that the residual of B1-14
was computed from.

**Severity: Low** (DoS). Not a new route past the budget: what changes is the
arithmetic of a residual already accepted. With 1.0, three connections at their
budgets and commands hold some 3 × (67 + 67) = 402 MB, past the packaged
384 MiB heap rather than about it (computed). The outcome is the one the owner
accepted at 1.62, the sidecar's out-of-memory exit with no disclosure, and it
needs the same hostile server driving all three connections while the user acts.

**Recommendation.** Either restate the ratio as some 1.0 and the residual with
it, or tighten the charge so a real margin returns: count an RFC 2231 value's
encoded form and charset as copies of their own, or charge strings at more than
one copy.

**Accepted for 0.1.0 by the owner (2026-10-08, 1.67)**, by the first way: the
ratio and the residual are restated (§4n), the outcome is unchanged, and the
budget per account planned after the release, one bound across an account's
folders and commands, closes this with B1-14's residual. A tighter charge per
shape is not pursued: the passes from 1.49 to 1.65 each found a shape the
estimate of the moment missed, and a bound per account does not rest on the
estimate's ratio.

**Status: accepted at 1.67.** Found by the pass over 1.65.

## 5. Informational notes (no change required)

- **Thread renumbering reads whole entities.**
  `ThreadingService.renumberThreadPositions` loads a thread's members through
  `findByAccountIdAndThreadId` as managed entities, and `MessageEntity.content`
  is a plain `@Lob` — eager by default — so the bodies come with them. It runs
  on every orphan merge and on a late arrival that sorts before an existing
  member, both of which a hostile server can provoke by drip-feeding a thread
  in descending date order. Two things bound it, and both were measured against
  the code rather than assumed. Each body's **read** is capped at 8 MiB by the
  B1-1 fix — the stored `content` can be several times that, because the
  plain-text escape and linkifying expand the text and inline images add their
  base64 (corrected at 1.9; the heap cost of that expansion, and of the Jsoup
  DOM for an 8 MiB HTML body, has not been measured). And — the larger
  effect — `content` is **null for a message nobody has
  opened**: the sync path persists through `MessageMapper.toEntity`, which
  has no body to set — the `FetchedMessage` it maps carries none — and
  `MessageContentPersister` is the only production code that writes `content`,
  on first open (re-checked at 1.9 by grepping `backend/src/main` for callers
  of `setContent(` and for JPQL or SQL writing the column; the comment 1.5
  quoted from the mapper is gone). The worst case is therefore
  a long thread the user has already read that the server keeps extending, not
  an arbitrary sync. Recorded rather than fixed: a body-free projection for the
  renumber would remove the heap term from both call sites and is worth its own
  change. Carried up from the freshness ledger at 1.5 — it was found during an
  acknowledgement, and acknowledgements are deleted at re-verification, so a
  note that only lived there would have been lost with them.

- **Attachment download is disk-bounded, not memory-bounded.**
  [AttachmentService.downloadToTempFile](../backend/src/main/java/org/voxrox/mailbackend/feature/mail/service/AttachmentService.java)
  streams the part to a private temp file via `Files.copy` (constant heap), with
  an empty-download integrity check and a stale-temp sweep on boot. A hostile
  server serving a huge attachment could fill the disk, but the copy never
  buffers the attachment in memory, the file lands in the app's own
  `<data-dir>\tmp` (1.0–1.8 said "the user's own temp dir"), and it is
  unlinked on stream close. Lower impact than B1-1; the same
  read-cap-and-reject approach would close it if ever desired. One lookup
  step before the copy was not disk-bounded until 1.18: a text part it
  descended through, or the body of a single-part text message, it read whole
  into memory — B1-12 (§4l, added at 1.16). It now asks only a multipart or an
  encapsulated message for its content, and since 1.34 only one Angus built from the structure it parsed, so no description of a part makes the lookup read it whole (B1-12; reopened at 1.24, which this note said nothing of until 1.31).

- **Attachment part lookup follows `message/rfc822` without a bound** (added
  at 1.9). `AttachmentService.findPartByPath` walks the stored part path, but
  unwraps an embedded message without consuming a path segment, so a server
  that serves a chain of nested `message/rfc822` at download time drives an
  unbounded recursion — the one MIME walk outside `MAX_DEPTH`. Low: it needs a
  user-initiated download and ends in a `StackOverflowError` on that request.
  Since 1.17 the chain is bounded all the same: the lookup walks the structure
  Angus parsed from a FETCH, and a FETCH nested past 256 levels is dropped
  (B1-10, §4j), so the recursion stays in the hundreds of frames; since 1.32 the levels are counted in Angus's parse itself, so no server hides them.

- **The `From` label decided the address used downstream** (added at 1.9,
  see §3; closed at 1.19). A personal name containing `<…>` became the
  allow-list key, the reply target, the reported `senderEmail` and a
  typeahead entry. Low: it moved the F2 image-loading trade-off onto an
  address the sender chose, and a prefilled reply is shown to the user before
  it is sent. B1-11's fix (§4k) reads the address from the label's last
  `<…>`, which `formatAddress` writes itself, in all four places, so a name
  no longer decides it.

- **A role read from a folder's name matches whole names only** (added at
  1.16). Without SPECIAL-USE, `ImapFolderService` takes a folder's role from its
  name through `FolderRole.fromNameFallback`, and until #580 any name
  containing a keyword qualified: "Robinson" contains "bin" and became the
  trash, where deletes then went, final for a message already in it;
  "Presentations" became Sent. The name now has to be a known system folder
  name as a whole (case, diacritics and whitespace aside, below at most one
  namespace prefix: `INBOX.`, `INBOX/`, `[Gmail]/` or `[Google Mail]/`), and each account pass reconciles the stored
  roles with the listing in both directions (`SyncStateService.reconcileRoles`,
  a targeted UPDATE of the role column, so it cannot write back a
  `last_known_uid` a concurrent cycle advanced). Recorded here, not as a
  finding: it needed a user's own folder name rather than a hostile server —
  which can name and mark its folders as it likes anyway — and it was fixed
  when found. `FolderRole` and `FolderSyncStateRepository` join `Code paths`
  at 1.16, since this note rests on them.
- **`ImapFolderExecutor` reads "not found" in any `MessagingException` as a
  missing folder** (added at 1.16). The executor classifies the exceptions an
  action throws by their text, and one that says "not found" becomes
  `ResourceNotFoundException` "Folder '…' was not found on the server",
  whatever was not found. Until #582 the attachment lookup fed it — a part
  number past the last part, and a single-part message's body, which the lookup
  could not find as part 1, both came back as a missing folder. The lookup now
  reports its own not-found and resolves part 1 as RFC 3501 numbers it; the
  heuristic itself is unchanged, so another action whose server error happens
  to say "not found" is still misreported. A correctness concern — the
  request fails either way, under the wrong name.
- **UIDVALIDITY is checked per sync cycle, not per operation** (added at 1.9,
  see §3). Low: acting on another message needs the server to renumber the
  mailbox between two cycles — which it can do at will, but only to its own
  mailbox's messages — and the next cycle resets the state. The draft-send
  path's hard delete of the sent draft is the case with the most effect.
- **The envelope's Message-ID is stored as Angus returns it** (added at
  1.64, by the pass over 1.63; read, not measured). `IMAPMessage.getMessageID`
  returns the envelope's string as it came, and `fetchBatch` stores it with
  no line-break handling, unlike `In-Reply-To` and `References` (1.45) and the
  subject (1.63). A server that writes a CR or LF into it, through a literal,
  would make every reply to the message unsavable, as both threading headers
  of the reply carry it. Functional, the class of §3b's notes; no honest
  server writes one. **Fixed at 1.66** (#667): `toFetchedMessage` removes CR
  and LF from the Message-ID with the pattern `getHeader` unfolds the
  threading headers with, so a reply's `In-Reply-To` and `References` carry it
  on one line and threading matches it against `References` stored unfolded.
  `IMAPMessage.getMessageID` returns `envelope.messageId` as read, and the
  raw header only when the whole message was fetched, which keeps a fold
  inside the value (both read from Angus 2.0.5). Two `MessageFetcherTest` cases
  fail against the 1.65 code, run and seen: a value with a line break, as an
  envelope literal gives it, and a header folded before a comment, which
  Jakarta Mail keeps folded. Not driven over a server: GreenMail builds its
  envelope from the header unfolded, and the reply's refusal of a line break
  is #645's, measured there. **Confirmed by the pass over 1.66** (1.67; a separate agent's, at
  `9f165eb5`): `getMessageID` returns `envelope.messageId`, read with
  `readString` and so raw from a literal, and the header only once the body is
  loaded; with the value stored as it comes, both tests fail, run and seen.
  Every other use of a Message-ID either reads the stored one or the id a draft
  is given here (`<uuid@voxrox.org>`), which carries no line break, the recipient
  check of §4e among them.
- **A reopened draft's attachment name is not checked for a line break**
  (added at 1.64, by the pass over 1.63; measured at the library level).
  `AttachmentRequest.fileName` has no line-break check, and for a reopened
  draft the name the client sends back is the one the server's copy gave;
  `MimeMessageBuilder` sets it with `setFileName(encodeText(…))`,
  which writes a CR LF inside the quoted name, so the line after it reads as a
  header of the part. The value is the server's own draft, so what it can
  inject is what it could write into the draft itself, the limit §4e already
  states.

## 7. Change log

- **1.68** (2026-10-08) — **§4e's residual of 1.40 fixed** (#670): a stored
  save, and a send, delete every earlier revision of the draft's chain the
  server still holds a row for, so a save or a send after a rejected autosave
  no longer leaves the last stored revision on the server as a second copy.
  Functional; the typed recipients are untouched and no claim of a finding
  changes. New tests in `DraftRecipientsChainIT` and
  `DraftRecipientsRepositoryIT` fail with each part of the fix removed, run and
  seen. Anchor unchanged at `6ff9ded`; the drift is acknowledged in
  `docs/audit-freshness.json`. Verdict unchanged, **PASS, one finding
  accepted**.
- **1.67** (2026-10-08) — **re-verified against `6ff9ded`**, clearing two
  acknowledgements, with a separate agent's pass over the fixes of 1.65 (B1-20
  and B1-14, #666) and 1.66 (the Message-ID, #667), read from the code at
  `db05b042` and `9f165eb5` and from Angus 2.0.5's and Jakarta Mail 2.1.5's
  bytecode, with every neutralization run again and `mvn clean verify` green.
  The delta from `77df058` under `Code paths` is exactly those two changes.
  Both hold. Corrections: a CR or an LF needs a weight of 4 and a space of 1 in
  the count, kept higher as an upper bound with room; the count bounds the
  rendering only for plainly named values. New is **B1-21** (Low, §4u): an RFC
  2231 parameter with a long charset keeps some 1.0 of its charge, not the 0.88
  B1-14's residual was computed from, so the three connections' 402 MB is past
  the 384 MiB heap rather than about it. **Accepted for 0.1.0 by the owner**,
  with the residual restated (§4n); the budget per account planned after the
  release closes both. Docs only; the javadocs that still say 0.66 and 0.88
  follow with the next change to their file. Verdict **PASS, one finding
  accepted**.
- **1.66** (2026-10-08) — **§5's Message-ID note fixed** (#667), as the pass
  over 1.63 found it: the sync stores the Message-ID with its line breaks
  removed, as it stores `In-Reply-To` and `References` since 1.45, so a reply
  to a message whose envelope carries one can be saved and sent. Functional;
  no claim of a finding changes. Two `MessageFetcherTest` cases fail against
  the 1.65 code, run and seen. Anchor unchanged at `77df058`; the drift is
  acknowledged in `docs/audit-freshness.json`. The fix joins 1.65's in waiting
  for the independent pass. Verdict unchanged, **all findings fixed,
  verification pending**.
- **1.65** (2026-10-08) — **B1-20 fixed, and B1-14 with it** (§4t, §4n,
  #666), by the recommendation's second way and a bound on the rendering
  rather than on the name: `partTypeChars` counts the most a part's type can
  render to, as Jakarta Mail writes a plainly named value; a FETCH with a part
  past 16,384 is dropped once parsed, and every part's count is charged as a
  copy. Measured, every parameter shape under the bound keeps at most 0.66 of
  its charge, the quotes 0.41, so the worst shape is 1.61's 0.88 again and the
  residual of 1.62 rests on its 59 MB again; an ordinary message is charged
  some 16.9 KB rather than 16.4. New tests drop the pass's shapes, check the
  count against `ContentType.toString` for fifteen values under three names,
  and charge the count; each neutralization fails them, run and seen, except
  a space or a tab counted once, which the segment characters absorb. The
  javadocs 1.64 corrected follow: `keptBytes`, `DEFAULT_OPEN_FOLDER_BUDGET`,
  `MAX_DRAFT_BYTES` and `BoundedImapMessage`. Anchor unchanged at `77df058`;

  at `64633fe`, the drift acknowledged in `docs/audit-freshness.json`.
- **1.36** (2026-10-05) — **B1-5 fixed again** (§4e, #632): a save marks the
  revision it replaces superseded by the stableId the client names, whatever
  the server answers, and current and superseded entries are bounded apart, so
  the user's autosaves no longer push out the entry of a draft the server
  hides. `draft_recipients` gains `stable_id` and `superseded_at` in
  `V1__init.sql`. Verdict: one open finding, B1-14. The independent
  verification pass is still to run. Anchor unchanged at `64633fe`, the drift
  acknowledged in `docs/audit-freshness.json`.
- **1.35** (2026-10-05) — **B1-15 fixed** (§4o, #631): Angus's parse of a
  FETCH is stopped once 64 calls in a row through `DepthBoundedFetchResponse`
  consume nothing, and the response dropped, which ends the body-extension
  loop. With it, the honest half of the group-marker residual that 1.33 added
  to §4j: markers are forgotten when their address list closes, so a message
  forwarding many messages with a group each is no longer dropped. The
  independent verification pass is still to run. Anchor unchanged at
  `64633fe`, the drift acknowledged in `docs/audit-freshness.json`.
- **1.34** (2026-10-05) — **B1-12 fixed again** (§4l, #630): the attachment
  lookup asks a part on the server for its content only when Angus built the
  parts from the structure it parsed, judged by the handler rather than the
  declared type, so a `multipart/*` in single-part form or a
  `message/rfc822` without an envelope is a leaf instead of a body read whole.
  §5's note follows. The independent verification pass is still to run.
  Anchor unchanged at `64633fe`, the drift acknowledged in
  `docs/audit-freshness.json`.
- **1.33** (2026-10-05) — **B1-10's fix confirmed; B1-15 found, so four
  findings are open** (#628). A separate agent's pass over 1.32, read from Angus
  2.0.5's sources jar rather than from the write-up, and measured with a
  throwaway test through `parseFetch` and a throwaway IT over TLS:
  - **B1-10 confirmed** (§4j). Every recursion of the FETCH parse starts with
    a counted `(`, every counted `)` but `BODY`'s one at the top ends a list
    the same frame opened, and data reaches the counted methods only as `(`
    bytes `skip` passes over, which count up. Nine bypass shapes around a
    structure 20,000 deep were all dropped. Honest messages lose their
    structure one way more than §4j said: group markers add up across every
    envelope in the response, so a message forwarding 126 messages that each
    name a group is dropped (100 parse); the residual says so.
  - **B1-15 found** (Medium, §4o, open; measured). `parseBodyExtension` loops
    without end on an extension element it cannot read, such as a bare atom:
    the account's sync thread spins at a full core, in the parse, holding the
    account, and a restart meets the same response.

  Anchor unchanged at `64633fe`; the acknowledgement of 1.32 stays in
  `docs/audit-freshness.json`.

- **1.32** (2026-10-05) — **B1-10 fixed again** (§4j), by counting the levels
  Angus's own parse of a FETCH opens rather than scanning the response before
  it: `DepthBoundedFetchResponse` overrides the `Response` methods the parse
  consumes structure through, and stops it past 256 levels. Reading 2.0.5's
  source for it found a second unbounded recursion, nested address groups in
  an `ENVELOPE`, which keeps the parentheses flat — 10,000 overflow the stack,
  measured — and the count covers it. §2, §3 and §5 follow. The independent
  verification pass is still to run. Anchor unchanged at `64633fe`, the drift
  acknowledged in `docs/audit-freshness.json`.
- **1.31** (2026-10-04) — **re-verified against `64633fe`, clearing all eight
  acknowledgements; two fixes reopened, so four findings are open** (#622).
  A separate agent's pass over the six fixes 1.25–1.30 recorded, re-derived
  from the code and Angus 2.0.5's bytecode rather than from their write-ups,
  with a throwaway IT over TLS where a claim is measured (method statement):
  - **B1-10 reopened** (Medium, §4j; measured). It settles the item 1.27 left
    to verify: the check does not read every FETCH item as Angus does. FLAGS
    reads its list raw to the first `)`, a NIL is one letter and two unread
    bytes, a literal needs no CRLF, and BODY consumes the byte after its `]`
    and after an origin, so a `"` in any of those places hides the nesting.
    Seven shapes, `FLAGS (")` among them, each end the pass in a
    `StackOverflowError` from the `BODYSTRUCTURE` constructor.
  - **B1-5 reopened** (Medium, §4e; read). The Message-ID key holds; the
    count does not, since a save forgets the replaced revision's entry only
    when it finds that revision's row, and a server that withholds APPENDUID
    decides that it does not, so the entries grow by saves and a thousand
    autosaves push out a hidden draft's entry.
  - **Confirmed**: B1-8 (§4h), B1-13 (§4m), B1-11 (§4k) and B1-14's honest
    half (§4n), each re-derived and its suites run. B1-12 and B1-14's hostile
    half stay open; the latter re-measured, 100,000 UID-table entries from one
    admitted command.

  Corrections: §4m's "a hundred thousand in a folder of 20,000" is some 46,000
  (the charge counts the expunged entries too; the constant's javadoc says

- **1.25** (2026-09-29) — **B1-8 fixed again, and B1-14 found** (#601): a

- **1.24** (2026-09-29) — **re-verified against `b606f97`, clearing all eight

- **1.23** (2026-09-28) — **B1-8's fix completed** (#598), by its author,
  reviewing it ahead of the verification pass. Two holes in 1.22's version: the
  budget sat in an override of `Protocol.command`, and Angus's authentication
  collects the responses to AUTHENTICATE in a loop of its own, so a server could
  flood the sign-in; and the UID listing and `CHANGEDSINCE`, read one response
  at a time, kept a UID or a change per response with nothing bounding how many.
  The budget now charges every untagged response in `readResponse` up to the
  tagged one, except a command `readEach` reads one at a time, and `readEach`
  hands on at most `MAX_MESSAGES` responses (§4h). The verdict stays **all
  findings fixed, verification pending**. Drift acknowledged, anchor unchanged
  at `f6ae132`.

- **1.22** (2026-09-28) — **B1-8 fixed** (#596), and with it every finding the

- **1.21** (2026-09-28) — **B1-9 fixed** (#595). The lazy page fetch stops at

- **1.20** (2026-09-28) — **B1-5 fixed again** (#594). The recipients the user

- **1.17** (2026-09-28) — **B1-10 fixed**. A FETCH whose parentheses nest
